Higher categories of push-pull spans, I: Construction and applications
Lorenzo Riva

Abstract
. This is the first part of a project aimed at formalizing Rozansky-Witten models in the functorial field theory framework. Motivated by work of Calaque-Haugseng-Scheimbauer, we construct a family of symmetric monoidal ( ∞ , 3 ) (\infty,3) -categories 𝒫 ​ 𝒫 ​ ( 𝒞 , Q ⊗ ) \mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes}) , parametrized by an ∞ \infty -category 𝒞 \mathscr{C} with finite limits and a representable functor 𝒬 ⊗ = 𝒞 ⁡ ( − , Q ⊗ ) : 𝒞 op → CAlg ⁡ ( 𝒞 ​ at 1 ) \mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}=\mathscr{C}(-,Q^{\otimes}):\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}) with pushforwards, which contains correspondences in 𝒞 \mathscr{C} with local systems in 𝒬 ⊗ \mathscr{Q}^{\otimes} that compose via a push-pull formula. We apply this general construction to provide an approximation 𝒞 ​ ℛ ​ 𝒲 \mathscr{CRW} to the 3 3 -category of Rozansky-Witten models whose existence was conjectured by Kapustin-Rozansky-Saulina; this approximation behaves like a “commutative” version of the conjectured 3 3 -category and is related to work of Stefanich on higher quasicoherent sheaves.



2000 Mathematics Subject Classification
18N65

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation

1.1.1.
Field theories and higher categories

There is an interesting interplay between algebraic topology and modern mathematical physics whose formalization goes back to the work of Atiyah in
[
Ati88
]
. The objects in question are
functorial topological field theories
: these are symmetric monoidal functors

F:(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ord}_{n},\sqcup)\to(\mathscr{C},\otimes)

from a certain
(\infty,n)
-category of bordisms, possibly with additional tangential or geometric structures, to a target symmetric monoidal
(\infty,n)
-category
\mathscr{C}
, usually a higher version of the category of vector spaces or chain complexes. In recent years there has been an explosion of interest in field theories due to their connection with the higher algebra of
\infty
-categories via the cobordism hypothesis (see
[
BD95
,
Lur09b
,
GP22
]
). The construction of functorial field theories starts by choosing or producing an appropriate target
(\infty,n)
-category
\mathscr{C}
; one can then attempt to directly define
F
, which is usually very complicated for
n\geq 2
, or study the fully dualizable objects of
\mathscr{C}
and then appeal to the cobordism hypothesis to guarantee the existence of
F
. Some examples of both techniques can be found in
[
Abr96
,
Cos07
,
SP09
,
ST11
,
Juh18
,
DSPS20
,
CHS22
,
BEP23
]
in a variety of low dimensional or non-extended settings.

Unfortunately,
(\infty,n)
-categories are notoriously hard to construct due to the difficulty in making the composition operations satisfy an infinite collection of homotopy coherence axioms. The step-by-step technique used for building weak
n
-categories, which consists in specifying the collection of
k
-morphisms and then verifying a small set of axioms, does not work anymore, and the situation is even more complicated when one wants to add extra structure such as a symmetric monoidal product. This is a major roadblock for the study of field theories: many higher categories of physical interest, which arise from the study of classical or quantum field theory, involve a complicated interplay of algebra, geometry, and topology; collecting together this mixture of different mathematical objects in one coherent structure is difficult given the current techniques for building higher categories. In this paper we will solve this problem in a specific case of interest.

1.1.2.
The Rozansky-Witten
3
-category

In
[
KRS09
]
Kapustin, Rozansky, and Saulina studied a
3
-dimensional field theory arising from physics which they call the
Rozansky-Witten model
. Its rich structure is connected to Kähler geometry, derived categories of coherent sheaves, matrix factorizations, and deformation quantization. Motivated by the results in
[
KRS09
]
, Kapustin and Rozansky set out to describe a symmetric monoidal
3
-category
\mathbf{RW}
1
1

1

In Kapustin and Rozansky’s notation
\mathbf{RW}
is
\dddot{\mathsf{L}}
. Given how we usually denote categories in this paper we prefer the more evocative notation of
[
BCR23
]
.
that would classify Rozansky-Witten models in the functorial field theory framework. In
[
KR10
]
they provide an extensive sketch of the construction:

(a)

the objects are holomorphic symplectic manifolds and the morphisms include (but are not restricted to) Lagrangian spans; there is a dualization operation

(X,\omega)\mapsto(X,\omega)^{\vee}:=(X,-\omega)

and a product

(X,\omega_{X})\odot(Y,\omega_{Y}):=(X\times Y,\pi_{X}^{\ast}\omega_{X}+\pi_{Y}^{\ast}\omega_{Y})

and we have

\mathbf{RW}(X,Y)\simeq\mathbf{RW}(\ast,X^{\vee}\odot Y)

for any symplectic manifolds
X
and
Y
, where
\ast
is the point with the trivial symplectic form;

(b)

when restricting to cotangent bundles
X=\mathbf{T}^{\ast}U
with their canonical symplectic structure, the category of
2
-morphisms
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{RW}(\ast,X)}(L_{1},L_{2})
between two Lagrangians
L_{1},L_{2}
in
X
is a deformation of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded derived category of the intersection of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, and in fact it restricts to matrix factorizations in the special affine case of
X=\mathbf{T}^{\ast}\mathbb{C}^{n}
; one then uses the fact that holomorphic symplectic manifolds have Darboux charts isomorphic to cotangent bundles to glue together these mapping categories in the case of an arbitrary
X\approx\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathbf{T}^{\ast}U_{i}
;

(c)

compositions are given by pullbacks at the level of Lagrangian spans and push-pull formulas (see
Section

1.2.1
) at the level of sheaves.

Some excellent progress in describing
\mathbf{RW}
already appears in the literature. Using the powerful framework of derived algebraic geometry, Calaque, Haugseng, and Scheimbauer provide a “semi-classical” approximation in
[
CHS22
]
in the form of an
(\infty,3)
-category of iterated Lagrangian spans of symplectic derived stacks; this generalizes classical Rozansky-Witten theory from
[
RW97
]
for algebraic targets but does not account for the derived categories in the
2
-morphisms. Brunner, Carqueville, and Roggenkamp obtain a bicategory of “affine Rozansky-Witten models” in
[
BCR23
]
by using a categorification of matrix factorizations, which have been well-studied since their introduction in
[
Eis80
]
and have many applications all throughout homological algebra; their construction should embed in the homotopy
2
-category of
\mathbf{RW}
. The aforementioned works, together with the sequel
[
BCFR23
]
to
[
BCR23
]
, also contain strong dualizability theorems, making the constructions excellent for field theoretic applications.

1.2.
Main results

Our first result is a general construction of a symmetric monoidal
(\infty,3)
-category
\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
, dependent on two parameters with minimal requirements. We then specialize this result to give another approximation to
\mathbf{RW}
and bridge the gap between
[
CHS22
]
and
[
BCR23
]
.

Fix an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
with finite limits and a “representable” functor
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}:\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1})
– by this we mean a functor
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}:\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{S})
(see
Section

1.4
for the notation or
Section

A.4
for our model of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories) such that there exists another functor
Q^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}
, the representing object, and an equivalence
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(-)_{\bullet,\bullet}\simeq\mathscr{C}(-,Q^{\otimes}_{\bullet,\bullet})
. We require that for any morphism
f:c\to d
in
\mathscr{C}
the induced symmetric monoidal functor
f^{\ast}:\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(d)\to\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(c)
has a (lax monoidal) right adjoint.

Theorem A
.

There exists a a symmetric monoidal
(\infty,3)
-category
\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
of “push-pull spans in
\mathscr{C}
valued in
Q^{\otimes}
” satisfying the following three analogues of properties (a), (b), and (c) above:

(a’)

the bottom categorical layer (
0
- and
1
-morphisms) is the
\infty
-category of spans in
\mathscr{C}
, whose objects are those of
\mathscr{C}
and morphisms are given by spans; the symmetric monoidal structure is induced by the product in
\mathscr{C}
and by the tensor product of the
\infty
-categories
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(-)
;

(b’)

given two spans
X\leftarrow Z_{i}\to Y
(
i=1,2
) the top categorical layer (
2
- and
3
-morphisms) is given by the
\infty
-category
\mathscr{Q}(Z_{1}\cap Z_{2})
, where
Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}=Z_{1}\times_{X\times Y}Z_{2}
and
\mathscr{Q}(-)
denotes the underlying
\infty
-category of
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(-)
;

(c’)

the composition operation is given by pullbacks in the bottom layer and by a push-pull formula (see
Section

1.2.1
for more details) in the top layer.

This result can be generalized to obtain an
(\infty,n+1)
-category where the first
n
levels are given by spans in
\mathscr{C}
and the
\infty
-categories of morphisms in the top levels are given by
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(-)
. The existence part of
Theorem

A
follows from
Theorem

2.31
and the properties follow directly from the construction.

We can apply
Theorem

A
to obtain the promised approximation to
\mathbf{RW}
. This is done in
Section

3.2
after some technical results at the end of
Section

2
.

Theorem B
.

Fix an algebraically closed field
\mathbb{K}
of characteristic
0
. Then there is a symmetric monoidal
(\infty,3)
-category
\mathscr{CRW}
whose objects are symplectic derived stacks over
\mathbb{K}
,
1
-morphisms are Lagrangian spans, and the
\infty
-category of
2
-morphisms between Lagrangian spans
X\leftarrow L_{i}\to Y
(
i=1,2
) is the
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded quasicoherent sheaves on the derived intersection
L_{1}\cap L_{2}
.

Note that
\mathscr{CRW}
depends on the field
\mathbb{K}
chosen as the base for our derived stacks. We call
\mathscr{CRW}
the
(\infty,3)
-category of commutative Rozansky-Witten theories
. Before adding the symplectic structure, if we restrict to
1
-affine
stacks (see
[
Ste21
]
) then the resulting hom
(\infty,2)
-categories from
X
to
Y
resemble the
(\infty,2)
-categories
\mathrm{2}\mathcal{Q}\mathrm{Coh}(X\times Y)
constructed by Stefanich in
[
Ste21
]
.

The reasoning behind the name comes from the discrepancy between predictions for the behavior of the hom categories in
\mathbf{RW}
and their actual behavior in
\mathscr{CRW}
. Concretely, fix
X=\mathbb{K}^{n}
and a polynomial map
p:X\to\mathbb{K}
. Then the derivative
\partial p
induces a Lagrangian submanifold
L_{p}\subseteq\mathbf{T}^{\ast}X
of the cotangent bundle of
X
, the
graph of
\partial p
, and it is expected that the
2
-morphisms in
\mathbf{RW}
between
L_{p}
and
L_{0}
, the graph of the zero polynomial, correspond to
matrix factorizations of
p
:

(1.2.1)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{RW}(X)}(L_{p},L_{0})\simeq\mathrm{MF}(\mathbb{K}[x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}];p).

Here
\mathbf{RW}(X):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{RW}}(\ast,\mathbf{T}^{\ast}X)
, where the cotangent bundle has its canonical symplectic form. From results of Dyckerhoff (see
[
Dyc11
]
) we know that in certain cases the right-hand side of
Equation

1.2.1
is equivalent to a category of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded dg-modules over a
non-commutative
dg-algebra. For example, in the very explicit case of
p(x)=x^{n+1}
(
n\geq 2
) we have a dg-algebra
(A,d)
defined by

A:=\mathbb{K}[x;\theta,\partial_{\theta}]/(\partial_{\theta}\theta=1,\theta^{2}=\partial_{\theta}^{2}=0),\quad dx=0,d\theta=x,d\partial_{\theta}=(n+1)x^{n}

with
x
in even degree and
\theta,\partial_{\theta}
in odd degree; then

\mathrm{MF}\left(\mathbb{K}[x];\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}\right)\simeq\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{A}.

On the other hand, the
\infty
-categories of
2
-morphisms in
\mathscr{CRW}
are modeled by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded dg-modules over
commutative
dg-algebras: using the same example,

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{CRW}(X)}(L_{p},L_{0})\simeq\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(L_{p}\cap L_{0})

where
L_{p}\cap L_{0}
is the derived critical locus of
p
, which can be explicitly calculated to be the spectrum of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded dg-algebra
(B,d)
defined by

B=\mathbb{K}[x;\varepsilon],\quad dx=0,d\varepsilon=x^{n}

with
x
in even degree and
\varepsilon
in odd degree; then

\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(L_{p}\cap L_{0})\simeq\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{B}.

One discrepancy between
\mathbf{RW}
and
\mathscr{CRW}
thus arises at least at the level of the extra non-commutative variables introduced by the matrix factorizations. We note that there is a map
B\to A
inducing a functor
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{A}\to\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{B}
, which suggests that we can pass from the
2
-morphisms in the non-commutative picture to the
2
-morphisms in the commutative picture – see
Section

1.2.2
for a generalization of this idea.

At the moment it is unclear how to overcome these differences, but we expect that it amounts to a careful manipulation of the geometric data in the local systems from
Theorem

A
. In particular, should there be a functorial way to encode all the desired geometric data that appears in the definitions of
[
KR10
, Sections 3, 4]
then our framework would immediately (or maybe with some minor modifications) allow us to get a better approximation of
\mathbf{RW}
.

1.2.1.
Aside: the push-pull formula and generalized spans

Assuming their well-definedness, the composition operations in
\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
and in
\mathscr{CRW}
are clear for at least some of the morphisms. The composition of two
1
-morphisms represented by spans in
\mathscr{C}
is given by pulling back along the common vertex:

The composition of two
3
-morphisms represented by arrows
f,g
in some
\infty
-categories
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(W_{1})
and
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(W_{2})
is given by pulling back those morphisms along the canonical maps and then composing them as arrows within some
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(W)
.

The composition of
2
-morphisms is trickier. Let’s say we have two objects
X,Y\in\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
, three
1
-morphisms
Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})}(X,Y)
seen as spans from
X
to
Y
, and two
2
-morphisms
\mathcal{M}\in\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(Z_{1}\cap Z_{2})
and
\mathcal{N}\in\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(Z_{2}\cap Z_{3})
. This information can all be summarized in the diagram in
Figure

1
, where
Z_{I}=\bigcap_{i\in I}Z_{i}
is the intersection (
=
appropriate pullback) of the
Z_{i}
s.

Z_{1}
Z_{2}
Z_{3}
Z_{1,2}
Z_{2,3}
Z_{1,3}
Z_{1,2,3}
\mathcal{M}
\mathcal{N}
?
f
g
h

Figure 1.
A “generalized span” encoding the push-pull composition of
2
-morphisms.

The diagram, which resembles the barycentric subdivision of a standard
2
-simplex, is what we call a “generalized span”. The composition
\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{M}
should occupy the spot taken by the question mark, and in fact we want it to be given by a
push-pull formula

(1.2.2)

\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{M}=h_{\ast}(f^{\ast}\mathcal{M}\otimes g^{\ast}\mathcal{N})\in\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(Z_{1}\cap Z_{3}).

Obviously we need to assume that
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}
has pushforwards in order for
h_{\ast}
to exist. It turns out that this assumption is enough if we also have the extra data contained in the generalized spans. While the formula seems easy at first glance, ensuring that it behaves coherently and that it presents the composition of
2
-morphisms in
\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
will take a non-trivial amount of combinatorics.

1.2.2.
Connections to matrix factorizations

Finally we note that, despite the discrepancy between the categories of
2
-morphisms described above in the discussion following
Theorem

B
, there is a strong connection between the
2
-category
\mathbf{MF}
2
2

2

Originally defined for matrix factorizations of polynomials over
\mathbb{C}
, this
2
-category and the results concerning it can be generalized to any algebraically closed field
\mathbb{K}
of characteristic
0
. We leave this implicit in the discussion.
of truncated affine Rozansky-Witten models studied by Brunner, Carqueville, Fragknos, and Roggenkamp in
[
BCR23
,
BCFR23
]
into the homotopy
2
-category of
\mathscr{CRW}
which precisely mimics the algebraic approach described in
[
KR10
, Section 1.3]
.
\mathbf{MF}
is expected to correctly model Rozansky-Witten field theories in the case of the affine cotangent bundles
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}\mathbb{C}^{n}
, so the following result gives a positive indication that
\mathscr{CRW}
is not far off from being a good model either:

Theorem

(Theorem A in
[
Riv24
]
)
.

There is a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathfrak{e}:\mathbf{MF}\to\mathbf{h}_{2}\mathscr{CRW}
whose essential image sits in the subcategory of
\mathbf{h}_{2}\mathscr{CRW}
spanned by the symplectic derived stacks of the form
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}\mathbb{K}^{n}
,
n\geq 0
.

The details appear in full in the sequel
[
Riv24
]
to this paper. It is important to note that the existence of
\mathfrak{e}
, together with the results of
[
BCR23
]
about the dualizable objects in
\mathbf{MF}
, implies that there are many non-trivial fully extended oriented
2
-dimensional field theories valued in
\mathbf{h}_{2}\mathscr{CRW}
. In fact, one can find the computations of those field theories in
[
Riv24
]
.

1.3.
Summary of the paper

The whole paper is written in the language of
\infty
-categories.
Appendix

A
contains the results we need in terms of higher categories, monoidal structures, miscellaneous techniques involving (co)ends and (co)cartesian fibrations, and other abstract machinery. We use
complete
n
-fold Segal spaces
as our model for
(\infty,n)
-categories (see
Section

A.2
for a quick review or
[
Bar05
]
for a more complete treatment), and in particular we use complete Segal spaces to model
\infty
-categories. It has been shown that all known models of
(\infty,n)
-categories – including complete
n
-fold Segal spaces – have the same homotopy theory; see
[
RV22
]
for
n=1
or
[
BR13
,
BR20
,
BSP21
]
for
n\geq 1
. In particular this implies that our main results do not depend on the choice of model we made.

The bulk of the construction is carried out in
Section

2
. We start with an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
with finite limits and a symmetric monoidal category object
Q^{\otimes}
valued in
\mathscr{C}
that satisfies a condition on the existence of the pushforwards determined by it (see
Assumption

2.24
). We modify Haugseng’s
(\infty,n)
-category of iterated spans in
\mathscr{C}
from
[
Hau18
]
to build a cocartesian fibration over
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}
which describes
generalized
iterated spans with local systems and a symmetric monoidal structure. The first
m
categorical levels are spans with local systems exactly as in
[
Hau18
]
:
1
-morphisms are spans in
\mathscr{C}
,
2
-morphisms are spans between spans in
\mathscr{C}
,
3
-morphisms are spans between spans between spans in
\mathscr{C}
, and so on. We take a different approach for the monoidal structure. The last
n
categorical levels contain diagrams which are modeled after barycentric subdivisions of a standard simplex and serve to setup the framework for the push-pull formulas to hold. The local systems on each generalized span associate specific algebraic objects at each categorical level. Creating these special local systems and proving that they assemble to a higher category is the content of
Section

2.4
. In the end we’ll have obtained a symmetric monoidal
(\infty,3)
-category
\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
of generalized spans with “push-pull” local systems. We then construct a relative version of
\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
dependent on a symmetric monoidal functor
U:\mathscr{D}^{\odot}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}
which allows us to talk about generalized
\odot
-cartesian spans in
\mathscr{D}
such that their image in
\mathscr{C}
under
U
has a push-pull local system.

In
Section

3
we provide some applications of the results of
Section

2
. First we review enough derived algebraic geometry to talk about the
\infty
-categories
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
of derived stacks and
\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}
of presymplectic derived stacks together with the functor
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}:\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1})
of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded quasicoherent sheaves. Then we turn the crank built in
Section

2
to obtain
\mathscr{CRW}
, our approximation to
\mathbf{RW}
.

1.4.
Notation

For the reader’s convenience we provide a list of objects that are commonly found in the literature or repeatedly used in this paper, together with some conventions that we will try to stick to.

Some notation concerning categories:

•

\mathbf{\Delta}
is a skeleton of the category of linearly ordered finite sets. Its objects are the sets
[n]=\{0\leq 1\leq\dotsb\leq n\}
, for
n
a non-negative integer, and morphisms are order-preserving functions. A moprhism
\varphi
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
is
inert
if
\varphi(i)=\varphi(0)+i
for any
i
in the domain of
\varphi
. The special inert morphisms

\rho_{i}:[1]\to[n],\quad(0,1)\mapsto(i,i+1)

are called the
Segal maps
. If
\varphi
is a map in
\mathbf{\Delta}
then we call the corresponding map in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
a
face map
if
\varphi
is injective and a
degeneracy map
if
\varphi
is surjective. Every map in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
can be factored uniquely as a face map followed by a degeneracy map.

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a skeleton of the category of pointed finite sets (i.e.
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is a skeleton of the
opposite
of pointed finite sets). Its objects are the pointed sets
\langle n\rangle=\{\ast,1,\dotsc,n\}
, for
n
a non-negative integer, and a morphism
\langle m\rangle\to\langle n\rangle
of pointed sets. This category has two symmetric monoidal structures, induced respectively by the wedge product
\vee
and the smash product
\wedge
of finite pointed sets. An
inert
morphism
\varphi
is one such that
\left\lvert\varphi^{-1}(j)\right\rvert=1
for any
j\neq\ast
in the codomain of
\varphi
; that is, an inert morphism
\varphi
is injective on
\dom(\varphi)\setminus\varphi^{-1}(\ast)
. The special inert morphisms

\tau_{i}:\langle n\rangle\to\langle 1\rangle,\quad k\mapsto\begin{cases}1&k=i,\\
\ast&k\neq i\end{cases}

are called the
Segal maps
.

•

\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{n}
is the
\infty
-category of
(\infty,n)
-categories modeled as complete
n
-fold Segal spaces – see
Definition

A.5
.

\circledast

\mathscr{S}\simeq\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{0}\subseteq\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
is the
\infty
-category spaces. The ordinary category
\mathbf{S}
of sets embeds into
\mathscr{S}
as the discrete spaces.

\circledast

\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}\subseteq\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
is the full subcategory containing the nerves of ordinary categories. When using an ordinary category we will implicitly pass to its nerve and consider it as an
\infty
-category. We stick to a general notational distinction: script calligraphic letters (
\mathscr{A}
,
\mathscr{B}
,
\mathscr{C}
, etcetera) will denote
\infty
-categories and bold letters (
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{B}
,
\mathbf{C}
, etcetera) will denote ordinary categories.

\circledast

\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{2}\subseteq\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{2}
is the category of categories enriched in small categories, embedded into
(\infty,2)
-categories via a binerve – see
Section

2.3.1
.

•

If
x\in\mathscr{C}
is an object we will use
\mathscr{C}_{/x}
and
\mathscr{C}_{x/}
to denote the overcategory of maps into
x
and the undercategory of maps from
x
, respectively.

•

We will reserve
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(-,-)
for the space of morphisms in an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
; in particular,
\mathrm{Map}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}}
. As usual, we denote by
\mathrm{Fun}(-,-)
the
\infty
-category of functors between two
\infty
-categories and by
\mathrm{Nat}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Fun}(-,-)}
the space of natural transformations between such functors.

•

\mathrm{LKan}
and
\mathrm{RKan}
denote left and right Kan extensions – see
[
Lur18a
, Section 7.3]
or
Section

A.3
for more details and formulas.

•

If
T\in\mathscr{S}
and
c\in\mathscr{C}
then we denote by

T\pitchfork c:=\lim\left(T\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{const}_{c}}\mathscr{C}\right),\quad T\otimes c:=\colim\left(T\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{const}_{c}}\mathscr{C}\right)

the cotensoring and tensoring of
c
by
T
whenever these (co)limits exist. They satisfy

\mathscr{C}(T\otimes c,d)\simeq\mathscr{S}(T,\mathscr{C}(c,d))\simeq\mathscr{C}(c,T\pitchfork d)

for
c,d\in\mathscr{C}
whenever these (co)limits exist.

•

The end and coend of a functor
F:\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}
(see
Section

A.3
for a definition) are denoted by

\int_{\mathscr{A}}F\quad\text{and}\quad\int^{\mathscr{A}}F

respectively.

Throughout the course of the paper we define or recall the definition of:

•

span-like posets:

\circledast

\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k}
,
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{k}
(
Definition

2.1
): the posets used to parametrize cartesian iterated spans;

\circledast

\mathbf{\Theta}^{l}
,
\mathbf{\Xi}^{l}
(
Definition

2.1
): the posets used to parametrize cartesian iterated barycentric subdivisions of standard simplices;

•

span fibrations and related categories:

\circledast

\mathrm{GS}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}
(
Definition

2.4
): generalized spans in
\mathscr{C}
;

\circledast

\mathrm{GS}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}
(
Definition

2.5
): generalized cartesian spans in
\mathscr{C}
;

\circledast

\mathrm{GS}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{P})^{m,n}
(
Definition

2.15
): generalized cartesian spans in
\mathscr{C}
with local systems induced by a functor
\mathcal{P}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})
;

\circledast

\mathrm{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
(
Definition

2.25
),
\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
(
Definition

2.32
): push-pull spans in
\mathscr{C}
valued in a symmetric monoidal category object
Q^{\otimes}
;

\circledast

\mathrm{PP}(U:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
(
Definition

2.34
),
\mathscr{PP}(U:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
(
Definition

2.36
): push-pull spans in
\mathscr{D}
valued in a symmetric monoidal category object
Q^{\otimes}
in
\mathscr{C}
;

\circledast

\mathscr{CRW}(\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp};Q^{\otimes})
(
Definition

3.2
),
\mathscr{CRW}
(
Corollary

3.7
): algebraic Rozansky-Witten
(\infty,3)
-category;

•

path category for push-pull local systems:

\circledast

\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
(
Definition

2.17
),
\mathfrak{d}[l]
(
Definition

2.18
): the path category of the poset
[l]
and its bisimplicial nerve;

\circledast

\mathfrak{d}X
(
Definition

2.19
): the path category labelled by a bisimplicial object
X
;

\circledast

\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}
(
Equation

2.3.7
): a heavy modification of a symmetric monoidal category object
Q^{\otimes}
in
\mathscr{C}
;

•

basics of derived symplectic algebraic geometry (
Section

3.1.1
,
Section

3.1.4
):

\circledast

\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathbb{K}}
,
\mathscr{CA}\mathrm{lg}_{\mathbb{K}}
: derived modules and derived commutative algebras;

\circledast

\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}
,
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
: derived affine stacks and derived stacks;

\circledast

\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{A}
,
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{A}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
: derived (
\mathbb{Z}
-graded) modules and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded modules over a derived algebra
A
;

\circledast

\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}(X)
,
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X)
,
\mathcal{O}_{X}
: quasicoherent sheaves and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded sheaves on a derived stack
X
, and the structure sheaf of
X
;

\circledast

\mathbb{L}_{X}
,
\mathbb{T}_{X}
: cotangent and tangent stack on
X
;

\circledast

\mathcal{A}^{p}(X)
,
\mathcal{A}^{p,\mathrm{cl}}(X)
:
p
-forms and closed
p
-forms on a derived stack
X
;

\circledast

\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}
: presymplectic derived stacks.

And finally some useful conventions:

•

Given any object
A
, repeated superscripts
(\dotsb((A^{r_{1}})^{r_{2}})\dotsb)^{r_{k}}
will be turned into a multi-superscript
A^{r_{1},r_{2},\dotsc,r_{k}}
with entries separated by commas; similarly for subscripts. We will only use parentheses to distinguish between possibly ambiguous meanings of the repeated super/subscripts. We also abbreviate
(A^{\times n})^{\mathrm{op}}
as
A^{n,{\mathrm{op}}}
.

•

When dealing with commutative monoids
M^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}
in a cartesian monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
we reserve the superscript
\otimes
for the functor out of
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
, which encodes the totality of the monoidal structure, and remove the superscript when we are interested in the underlying object of
M^{\otimes}
, namely
M=M^{\otimes}_{1}
.

•

If
F:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
is a functor, the domain
\infty
-category of a (cartesian or cocartesian) fibration classified by it will be denoted by
\widehat{F}
– see
Section

A.1
.

•

We use
\simeq
for equivalence within an
\infty
-category and
\cong
for isomorphism within an ordinary category, and we try to reserve the equality symbol
=
for definitional equalities.

1.5.
Acknowledgements

This work was greatly improved by many helpful conversations with friends and colleagues. Amongst them, we would like to acknowledge Chris Schommer-Pries and Stephan Stolz for being patient, insightful, and supportive mentors, and for suggesting
[
CHS22
]
as a starting point for this project; Nils Carqueville for providing early feedback on the strategy; Damien Calaque, Rune Haugseng, and Claudia Scheimbauer for writing
[
CHS22
]
and greatly influencing the writing style of this work; Adam Dauser for providing feedback, alternative constructions, and “reality checks” during the middle and last stages of writing the paper; Connor Malin and Mark Behrens for graciously offering their intuition on various homotopy-theoretic intricacies; Justin Hilburn for helpful comments regarding the connections with Rozansky-Witten theories; and all the speakers and participants of the conference “Higher Invariants in Functorial Field Theory” that took place in Regensburg in the summer of 2023, who provided a stimulating environment within which the crucial steps of
Section

2.4
were carried out.

2.
Construction of the
(\infty,3)
-category of push pull spans

Throughout the whole section we assume that
\mathscr{C}
is an
\infty
-category with finite limits. We will construct
\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
in steps. First we define a higher category of generalized cartesian spans with local systems similar to the one in
[
Hau18
]
and then we craft the local system that we will use to describe push-pull spans. Finally we put everything together and force the push-pull formulas to hold, obtaining an
(\infty,3)
-category. All of this will be done coherently with respect to the natural cartesian monoidal structure
\mathscr{C}^{\times}
on
\mathscr{C}
, which essentially goes along for the ride, so that in the end our
(\infty,3)
-category will have a symmetric monoidal structure as well. We conclude by describing relative push-pull spans associated to a symmetric monoidal functor
U:\mathscr{D}^{\odot}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}
for
\mathscr{D}^{\odot}
another symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category.

2.1.
The generalized span fibration

In this section we will obtain a cocartesian fibration of “generalized iterated spans” in
\mathscr{C}
.

2.1.1.
Indexing categories

Recall that an
inert morphism
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
is the inclusion of a connected subinterval, i.e. a function
\varphi:[m]\to[n]
such that
\varphi(i)=\varphi(0)+i
for
0\leq i\leq m
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{int}}
and
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{inj}}
denote the subcategories of
\mathbf{\Delta}
containing the inert and the injective maps, respectively. Then define

\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n}:=(\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{int},/[n]})^{\mathrm{op}},\quad\mathbf{\Theta}^{n}:=(\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{inj},/[n]})^{\mathrm{op}}.

There are two special full subcategories

\mathbf{\Lambda}^{n}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n},\quad\mathbf{\Xi}^{n}\subseteq\mathbf{\Theta}^{n}

spanned by the inert maps
(\varphi:[i]\to[n])\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n}
with source
[i]=[0],[1]
and the injective maps
(\psi:[i]\to[n])\in\mathbf{\Theta}^{n}
with source
[i]=[0]
, respectively.

Proposition 2.2
.

The associations
[n]\mapsto\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n}
and
[n]\mapsto\mathbf{\Theta}^{n}
are functorial in
\mathbf{\Delta}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha:[m]\to[n]
be a map in
\mathbf{\Delta}
.

If
\varphi:[i]\to[m]
is inert then there is an inert map
\alpha_{\ast}(\varphi):[\alpha(\varphi(i))-\alpha(\varphi(0))]\to[n]
defined by
\alpha_{\ast}(\varphi)(0)=\alpha(\varphi(0))
. Moreover, if
\varphi
and
\psi
are inert and fit into a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[i]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[j]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

in
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{int},/[m]}
– which, if there is one, is the
unique
such commutative diagram – then there is a inert map
\alpha_{\ast}(\chi)
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\alpha(\varphi(i))-\alpha(\varphi(0))]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\alpha(\psi(j))-\alpha(\psi(0))]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\ast}(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\ast}(\chi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\ast}(\psi)}

commute, specified by

\alpha_{\ast}(\chi)(0)=\alpha(\psi(0)+\chi(0))-\alpha(\psi(0))=\alpha(\varphi(0))-\alpha(\psi(0)).

It is easy to see from the formula for
\alpha_{\ast}(\chi)
that
\alpha_{\ast}
commutes with composition of morphisms in
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{int},/[m]}
. Hence
\alpha_{\ast}:\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{int},/[m]}\to\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{int},/[n]}
is a functor, and so after taking opposites we obtain a functor
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet}:\mathbf{\Delta}\to\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
.

If
\varphi:[i]\to[m]
is injective then there is a unique
k_{\varphi}\in\mathbb{N}
and a unique isomorphism
[k_{\varphi}]\cong\im(\alpha\circ\varphi)
of linearly ordered sets. Define
\alpha_{\ast}(\varphi)
to be the injection
[k_{\varphi}]\cong\im(\alpha\circ\varphi)\hookrightarrow[n]
. If
\varphi
and
\psi
are injective and fit into a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[i]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[j]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

in
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{inj},/[m]}
then there is an injection
\alpha_{\ast}(\chi)
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k_{\varphi}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k_{\psi}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\ast}(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\ast}(\chi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\ast}(\psi)}

commute, defined by

\alpha_{\ast}(\chi):[k_{\varphi}]\cong\im(\alpha\circ\varphi)=\im(\alpha\circ\psi\circ\chi)\hookrightarrow\im(\alpha\circ\psi)\cong[k_{\psi}].

If
\chi=\chi_{2}\circ\chi_{1}
then
\im(\alpha\circ\tau\circ\chi)\hookrightarrow\im(\alpha\circ\tau)
factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\im(\alpha\circ\tau\circ\chi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\im(\alpha\circ\tau\circ\chi_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\im(\alpha\circ\tau)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k_{\varphi}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k_{\psi}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k_{\tau}]}}

\cong

\cong

\cong

and so
\alpha_{\ast}
commutes with composition. Hence it is a functor
\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{inj},/[m]}\to\mathbf{\Delta}_{\mathrm{inj},/[n]}
and so, after taking opposites, we have a functor
\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet}:\mathbf{\Delta}\to\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
.
∎

The category
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n}
is an easily visualizable poset. For example, here is the case
n=3
(with
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{3}
spanned by the red asterisks):

\ast
\ast
\ast
\ast
\ast
\ast
\ast
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{3}:

On a pyramid of height
n
and base
n+1
, an element
(\varphi:[i]\to[n])\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n}
is represented by a dot at height
i
and positioned at
\varphi(0)
units from the left. Each dot at height
i
has two arrows going down to height
i-1
which correspond to the restriction of an inert map
\varphi:[i]\to[n]
to its only two subintervals isomorphic to
[i-1]
, namely
\{0,\dotsc,i-1\}
and
\{1,\dotsc,i-1\}
.

The category
\mathbf{\Theta}^{n}
has a nice geometric interpretation as well: its objects are the vertices of the barycentric subdivision of the standard
n
-simplex
\Delta^{n}
. Each object
[i]\hookrightarrow[n]
corresponds to a distinct embedding of the
i
-simplex
\Delta^{i}
into
\Delta^{n}
. The subcategory
\mathbf{\Xi}^{n}
consists of all vertices of
\Delta^{n}
, i.e. the embeddings
\Delta^{0}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}
. For small values of
n
,
\mathbf{\Theta}^{n}
and
\mathbf{\Xi}^{n}
(spanned by the red asterisks) look like this:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ast}
\mathbf{\Theta}^{0}:
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ast}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ast}
\bullet
\mathbf{\Theta}^{1}:
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ast}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ast}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ast}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\mathbf{\Theta}^{2}:

The arrows denote restrictions along smaller faces. We can represent the elements of
\mathbf{\Theta}^{n}
as tuples of integers: an injection
\varphi:[i]\to[n]
is equivalently an
(i+1)
-tuple
(r_{0},\dotsc,r_{i})
of strictly increasing positive integers from
[n]
. The
1
-tuples
(r_{0})
correspond to elements of
\mathbf{\Xi}^{n}
, and when
\alpha:[m]\to[n]
is a map in
\mathbf{\Delta}
the corresponding pushforward
\alpha_{\ast}:\mathbf{\Theta}^{m}\to\mathbf{\Theta}^{n}
sends a tuple
(r_{0},\dotsc,r_{i})
to the tuple
(\alpha(r_{0}),\dotsc,\alpha(r_{i}))
where we remove repeated elements - that is, we first pass to the
set

\{\alpha(r_{0}),\dotsc,\alpha(r_{i})\}
and then turn it into a tuple of size possibly smaller than
i+1
.

Note that there is an embedding
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{\Theta}^{n}
since every inert map is an injection. In view of this embedding we will think of
\mathbf{\Theta}^{n}
as a generalization of
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n}
.

2.1.2.
Generalized spans

Here is our main object of study for this section:

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be an
\infty
-category. A
generalized span
valued in
\mathscr{D}
is a functor

F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{m}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{l_{1},\dotsc,l_{n}}=\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k_{1}}\times\dotsb\times\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k_{m}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{l_{1}}\times\dotsb\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{l_{n}}\to\mathscr{D}.

Using the terminology of Haugseng, (iterated) spans valued in
\mathscr{D}
are functors
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{m}}\to\mathscr{D}
. Since
\mathbf{\Theta}^{p}
is a generalization of
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{p}
we allow ourselves to think of functors

\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{m}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{l_{1},\dotsc,l_{n}}\to\mathscr{D}

as (iterated) generalized spans. When no confusion can arise we will just refer to these objects as
spans
or
generalized spans
, and when the indices don’t play much of a role we will abbreviate the tuples
(k_{1},\dotsc,k_{m})
and
(l_{1},\dotsc,l_{n})
as
\overline{k}
and
\overline{l}
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\mathscr{D}^{\odot}
be a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category. The functor

\mathrm{GS}(\mathscr{D}^{\odot})^{m,n}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}

defined by

(\langle t\rangle,[k_{1}],\dotsc,[k_{m}],[l_{1}],\dotsc,[l_{n}])\mapsto\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{m}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{l_{1},\dotsc,l_{n}},\mathscr{D}^{\odot}_{t})

unstraightens to a cocartesian fibration
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D}^{\odot})^{m,n}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}
called the
generalized span fibration
or just the
span fibration
for short.

We will need this general construction for
Definition

2.34
but until then we will only concentrate only on the case where
\mathscr{D}^{\odot}=\mathscr{C}^{\times}
is the cartesian monoidal category
\mathscr{C}
– see
Section

A.4
– which is the easiest setting where
Definition

2.5
makes sense. We will abbreviate
\mathrm{GS}(\mathscr{C}^{\times})
as
\mathrm{GS}(\mathscr{C})
from now on.

Recall that
\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}\simeq\mathscr{C}^{\times t}
has finite limits by
Proposition

A.17
, and recall from
Section

A.1
that the elements of
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}
are precisely the generalized spans
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{C}_{t}
.

Definition 2.5
.

A span
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
is
cartesian
if
F
is a right Kan extension of its restriction to the subcategory

\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Xi}^{\overline{l}}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{m}}\times\mathbf{\Xi}^{l_{1},\dotsc,l_{n}}:=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{k_{1}}\times\dotsb\times\mathbf{\Lambda}^{k_{m}}\times\mathbf{\Xi}^{l_{1}}\times\dotsb\times\mathbf{\Xi}^{l_{n}}.

Explicitly, using the formula for right Kan extensions (see for example
Equation

A.3.2
),
F
is cartesian if the canonical map

F(\overline{x},\overline{y})\to\lim_{(\overline{u},\overline{v})\in(\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Xi}^{\overline{l}})_{/(\overline{x},\overline{y})}}F(\overline{u},\overline{v})

is an equivalence for every
(\overline{x},\overline{y})\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}
. Let
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}
denote the full subcategory of the span fibration spanned by the cartesian spans.

In the case
(m,n)=(1,0)
, a cartesian span valued in
\mathscr{D}
looks like a pyramid of spans in
\mathscr{D}
such that the top vertices of the spans in the
(i+1)
th row are obtained by taking pullbacks of the vertices in the
i
th row, for all
i\geq 1
. For example, when
k_{1}=3
the model cartesian span looks like
Figure

2
. In the desired
(\infty,1)
-category of spans this means that composition of morphisms (the spans in the second row from the bottom) is obtained by taking pullbacks.

A_{0}
A_{1}
A_{2}
A_{3}
A_{01}
A_{12}
A_{23}
A_{01}\times_{A_{1}}A_{12}
A_{12}\times_{A_{2}}A_{23}
A_{01}\times_{A_{1}}A_{12}\times_{A_{2}}A_{23}

Figure 2.
A cartesian span
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{3}\to\mathscr{D}
. Each square is a pullback.

When
m\geq 1
we have such pyramids of spans in
m
possible directions and each kind of composition is carried out by taking pullbacks.

In the case
(m,n)=(0,1)
, a cartesian span looks like an
l_{1}
-simplex each of whose
k
-dimensional faces is labelled by an object
B_{i_{0}}\times\dotsb\times B_{i_{k}}\in\mathscr{D}
corresponding to the product of the elements
B_{i}\in\mathscr{D}
associated to the vertices of the simplex. For example, when
l_{1}=2
the model generalized cartesian span looks like the diagram in
Figure

3
.

\mathbf{\Theta}^{2}\to\mathscr{D}
. Each

-dimensional face, for
