, is a limit diagram.

2.1.3.
Cartesian spans are functorial

We ultimately want to prove that cartesian spans are assemble to a functor. More precisely, so as to invoke straightening-unsraightening we want exactly
Corollary

2.12
, which states that the projection
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}
is a cocartesian fibration. The proof is split in three parts: for each morphism
\varphi
in one of the three factors
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
,
\mathbf{\Delta}^{m,{\mathrm{op}}}
, and
\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,{\mathrm{op}}}
we will show that
\varphi
has a cocartesian lift in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}
. The arguments used for each factor are different
3
3

3

The terminology is different as well and we hope the reader will not mind. For the first factor we talk about pushing forward by a map in
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
, while for the second and third factors we talk about pulling back by maps in
\mathbf{\Delta}
. This is because a span
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
gets pulled back along maps to its domain and pushed forward along maps from its codomain.
since they parametrize different kinds of spans.

The first factor:
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Since
\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}\simeq\mathscr{C}^{\times t}
has a canonical cartesian monoidal structure where products are computed componentwise (
Proposition

A.17
) we can measure the cartesian-ness of a span
F
by passing to its components:

Lemma 2.6
.

A generalized span
F
valued in
\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
is cartesian if and only if each component
F_{i}
, obtained by composing
F
with the Segal map
(\tau_{i})_{\ast}:\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}\to\mathscr{C}
, is cartesian.

Proof.

Limits in
\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
are computed componentwise, meaning that
x\in\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
is a limit of a diagram
D:\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}_{t}
if and only if the image of
x
under the Segal equivalence
\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}\simeq\mathscr{C}^{\times t}
is a limit of the composite diagram
\mathscr{A}\xlongrightarrow{D}\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times t}
– see part of the proof of
Proposition

A.17
. The claim then follows because right Kan extensions are limits.
∎

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{s}
be a cartesian span and let
\psi:\langle s\rangle\to\langle t\rangle
be a morphism in
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Then
\psi_{\ast}F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
is a cartesian span and there is a cocartesian arrow
F\to\psi_{\ast}F
in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}
sitting over
\psi
.

Proof.

There is a canonical cartesian arrow
F\to\psi_{\ast}F
in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}
, so it remains to be proven that
\psi_{\ast}F
is a cartesian span. We have the following commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}^{\times s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}^{\times t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\psi}}

where the
i
th component of
\pi_{\psi}
, for
1\leq i\leq t
, is given by

(c_{1},\dotsc,c_{s})\mapsto\prod_{k\in\psi^{-1}(i)}c_{k}.

Therefore the
i
th component of
\psi_{\ast}F
is

(\tau_{i})_{\ast}(\psi_{\ast}F)\simeq\prod_{k\in\psi^{-1}(i)}(\tau_{k})_{\ast}F\simeq\prod_{k\in\psi^{-1}(i)}F_{k}.

Since right Kan extensions commute with products we see that
(\tau_{i})_{\ast}(\psi_{\ast}F)
is a cartesian span in
\mathscr{C}
for every
i
, and so
\psi_{\ast}F
is a cartesian span in
\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
by
Lemma

2.6
.
∎

The second factor:
\mathbf{\Delta}^{m,{\mathrm{op}}}
.
We start with a useful lemma.

Lemma 2.8

(Generalization of
[
Hau18
, Lemma 5.6]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{A}_{0}\subseteq\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathscr{B}
denote two inclusions of
\infty
-categories and let
F:\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{D}
be a functor into another
\infty
-category with finite limits. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

F
is a right Kan extension of
F|_{\mathscr{A}_{0}\times\mathscr{B}_{0}}
;

(2)

F
is a right Kan extension of
F|_{\mathscr{A}_{0}\times\mathscr{B}}
and for every
a_{0}\in\mathscr{A}_{0}
the restriction
F|_{\{a_{0}\}\times\mathscr{B}}:\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{D}
is a right Kan extension of
F|_{\{a_{0}\}\times\mathscr{B}_{0}}
;

(3)

the mate
F^{\prime}:\mathscr{A}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})
is a right Kan extension of
F^{\prime}|_{\mathscr{A}_{0}}
and for every
a_{0}\in\mathscr{A}_{0}
the functor
F^{\prime}(a_{0}):\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{D}
is a right Kan extension of
F^{\prime}(a_{0})|_{\mathscr{B}_{0}}
.

Proof.

The arguments of the proof in
[
Hau18
]
for functors
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k}}\to\mathscr{D}
with
k=2
,
\mathscr{A}=\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n_{1}}
,
\mathscr{B}=\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n_{2}}
,
\mathscr{A}_{0}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{n_{1}}
and
\mathscr{B}_{0}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{n_{2}}
do not depend on the values of
\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B},\mathscr{A}_{0},\mathscr{B}_{0}
, and so the proof carries over identically for this more general case.
∎

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be an
\infty
-category with finite limits. If
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{D}
is a cartesian span and
\overline{\alpha}:[\overline{k^{\prime}}]\to[\overline{k}]
is a morphism in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\times m}
, then

(\overline{\alpha},\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{l}]})^{\ast}F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k^{\prime}}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{D}

is also a cartesian span and there is a cocartesian arrow
F\to(\overline{\alpha},\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{l}]})^{\ast}F
in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}
sitting over
(\overline{\alpha},\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{l}]})
.

Proof.

There is a cocartesian arrow
F\to(\overline{\alpha},\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{l}]})^{\ast}F
in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D})^{m,n}
, so we just have to prove that
(\overline{\alpha},\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{l}]})^{\ast}F
is a cartesian span. By applying
Lemma

2.8
to adjoin over the
\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}
factor we get that (A) the mate

F^{\prime}:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}},\mathscr{D})

is cartesian and that (B) each image
F^{\prime}(\overline{x}):\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{D}
is cartesian, for
\overline{x}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}
. From (A),
[
Hau18
, Proposition 5.9]
allows us to conclude that

\overline{\alpha}^{\ast}F^{\prime}:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k^{\prime}}}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}},\mathscr{D})

is cartesian. For every
\overline{y}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k^{\prime}}}
, if we set
\overline{x}=\overline{\alpha}_{\ast}\overline{y}\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}
we see that

\overline{\alpha}^{\ast}F^{\prime}(\overline{y})\simeq F(\overline{x})

is cartesian by (B).
Lemma

2.8
then guarantees that the mate to
\overline{\alpha}^{\ast}F^{\prime}
, which is precisely
(\overline{\alpha},\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{l}]})^{\ast}F
, is cartesian.
∎

The third factor:
\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,{\mathrm{op}}}
.
Pulling back the
\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}
factors along maps in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\times n}
does
not
preserve cartesian-ness, in general. In the simple case of
n=1
, if
F:\mathbf{\Theta}^{l}\to\mathscr{D}
is a cartesian span and
\varphi
is an
i
-face of
\Delta^{l}
then
F(\varphi)
is a product of
i+1
many terms which are all possibly distinct. When
\beta:[l^{\prime}]\to[l]
is not injective, say
\beta(a)=\beta(b)
, the pullback of
F
along
\psi
will mess up those faces of
\Delta^{l^{\prime}}
containing the vertices
a
and
b
: explicitly, if
\varphi:[1]\to[l^{\prime}]
is the embedding selecting
\{a,b\}\subseteq[l^{\prime}]
and
\nu_{a},\nu_{b}:[0]\to[l^{\prime}]
select
\{a\},\{b\}\subseteq[l^{\prime}]
respectively, then

\displaystyle\beta^{\ast}F(\nu_{x})

\displaystyle\simeq F(\nu_{x})\text{ for $x=a,b$},

\displaystyle\beta^{\ast}F(\varphi)

\displaystyle\simeq F(\nu_{a})\not\simeq F(\nu_{a})\times F(\nu_{b}).

Fortunately, there is a fix.

Definition 2.10
.

Given an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{D}
with finite limits and a generalized span
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{D}
, the
cartesian replacement

\mathrm{cr}(F)
is the span
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{D}
defined by

\mathrm{cr}(F):=\mathrm{RKan}_{I}(F\circ I),

where
I
is the inclusion
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Xi}^{\overline{l}}\subseteq\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}
.

Since
I
is fully faithful we have
\mathrm{cr}(F)\circ I\simeq F\circ I
(see the dual of
[
Lur18a
, Corollary 7.3.2.7]
) and so we immediately see that
\mathrm{cr}(F)
is a cartesian span, justifying the name:

\mathrm{RKan}_{I}(\mathrm{cr}(F)\circ I)\simeq\mathrm{RKan}_{I}(F\circ I)=\mathrm{cr}(F).

If
F
is cartesian then obviously
\mathrm{cr}(F)\simeq F
. Moreover, the canonical natural transformation
F\to\mathrm{cr}(F)
(obtained as the unit of
I^{\ast}\dashv\mathrm{RKan}_{I}
) determines an arrow
F\to\mathrm{cr}(F)
in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D})^{m,n}
sitting over the identity morphism of
([\overline{k}],[\overline{l}])
in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\times(m+n)}
with the following property: if
F\to G
is an arrow in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D})^{m,n}
then there is an essentially unique arrow
\mathrm{cr}(F)\to\mathrm{cr}(G)
in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D})^{m,n}
making the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}(G)}

commute. This follows immediately from the description of cartesian replacements as right Kan extensions.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be an
\infty
-category with finite limits, let
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{D}
be a cartesian generalized span, and let
\overline{\beta}:[\overline{l^{\prime}}]\to[\overline{l}]
be a morphism in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\times n}
. Then the arrow
f:F\to\mathrm{cr}((\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{k}]},\overline{\beta})^{\ast}F)
in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}
obtained by composing the two arrows

F\to(\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{k}]},\overline{\beta})^{\ast}F,\quad(\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{k}]},\overline{\beta})^{\ast}F\to\mathrm{cr}((\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{k}]},\overline{\beta})^{\ast}F)

in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D})^{m,n}
is a cocartesian arrow sitting over
(\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{k}]},\overline{\beta})
.

Proof.

Set
G:=\mathrm{cr}((\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{k}]},\overline{\beta})^{\ast}F)
. Let
(\overline{\gamma},\overline{\delta}):([\overline{k}^{\prime}],[\overline{l}^{\prime\prime}])\to([\overline{k}],[\overline{l}^{\prime}])
be another map in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\times(m+n)}
and let
g:F\to K
be an arrow in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}
sitting over
(\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{k}]},\overline{\beta})\circ(\overline{\gamma},\overline{\delta})=(\overline{\gamma},\overline{\beta\circ\delta})
. To prove that
f
is a cocartesian arrow we must show that there is an essentially unique arrow
h:G\to K
such that
h\circ f\simeq g
. The construction of
h
is outlined in this commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{k}]},\beta)^{\ast}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{\gamma},\overline{\beta\circ\delta})^{\ast}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cr}(K)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}

The vertical arrows in the large rectangle are the cartesian replacements and
H=\mathrm{cr}((\overline{\gamma},\overline{\beta\circ\delta})^{\ast}F)
. The arrows
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are uniquely determined by the universal property of the cartesian replacement. The top right triangle commutes by a basic property of cocartesian fibrations, see
Section

A.1
. Finally, the right vertical arrow is an equivalence because
K
is a cartesian span.
∎

We can combine these three propositions to prove the following:

Corollary 2.12
.

The projection
\pi:\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}
is a cocartesian fibration.

Proof.

If we start with a cartesian span
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{s}
and morphisms

\psi:\langle s\rangle\to\langle t\rangle,\quad\overline{\alpha}:[\overline{k^{\prime}}]\to[\overline{k}],\quad\overline{\beta}:[\overline{l^{\prime}}]\to[\overline{l}]

in
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
,
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\times m}
, and
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\times n}
, respectively, then proving the claim amounts to showing that there is a cartesian span
G:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}^{\prime}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}^{\prime}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
and a cocartesian morphism
F\to G
in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}
. We can find this chain of cocartesian morphisms in
\\
what{GS}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}
:

(1)

F\to F^{\prime}:=\psi_{\ast}F
, where
F^{\prime}
is a cartesian span by
Proposition

2.7
;

(2)

F^{\prime}\to F^{\prime\prime}:=(\overline{\alpha},\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{l}]})^{\ast}F^{\prime}
, where
F^{\prime\prime}
is a cartesian span by
Proposition

2.9
;

(3)

F^{\prime\prime}\to G:=\mathrm{cr}((\mathrm{id}_{[\overline{k^{\prime}}]},\overline{\beta})^{\ast}F^{\prime\prime})
, where
G
is a cartesian span by definition and the arrow is cocartesian by
Proposition

2.11
.

The composition of the above morphisms gives a cocartesian morphism
F\to G
, as desired.
∎

2.2.
Generalized spans with local systems

In this section we will extend the fibration
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}
classifying generalized spans to a fibration
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}_{/\!\!/\sigma}
that classifies generalized spans with local systems valued in some section
\sigma
of
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}
. The specific
\sigma
we intend to use will be constructed later, but here is the general idea:

Say
P\in\mathscr{C}
. A
P
-valued local system on an object
X\in\mathscr{C}
is simply a map
X\to P
. We think of
P
as a classifying object of some sort, much like
BG
classifies principal
G
-bundles in
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
classifies cocartesian fibrations in a large
\infty
-category of
\infty
-categories. Now assume
P
is a functor
\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{E}
. A
P
-valued local system on a functor
F:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{E}
is a natural transformation
F\to P
of functors. In the setting of generalized spans,

•

\mathscr{D}
is
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}
,

•

\mathscr{E}
is
\mathscr{C}_{t}^{\times}
,

•

and the section
\sigma
picks out a particular span
P_{t,\overline{k},\overline{l}}:=\sigma(\langle t\rangle,[\overline{k}],[\overline{l}]):\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
for every element
(\langle t\rangle,[\overline{k}],[\overline{l}])\in\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n}

Then a vertex of
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}_{/\!\!/\sigma}
will be exactly a generalized span
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{C}_{t}^{\times}
together with a natural transformation
F\to P_{t,\overline{k},\overline{l}}
.

2.2.1.
From functors to sections

We start with a useful proposition.

Proposition 2.13

(Generalization of
[
GHN17
, Proposition 7.3]
)
.

Let
F:\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
and
G:\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
be functors which correspond to a cartesian fibration
\widehat{F}\to\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}
and a cocartesian fibration
\widehat{G}\to\mathscr{B}
. Define
S:\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
by
S(a,b)=\mathrm{Fun}(F(a),G(b))
and let
\pi:\widehat{S}\to\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}
be a corresponding cocartesian fibration. Then for any functor
H:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}
we have an equivalence between the space of functors
\mathscr{D}\to\widehat{S}
over
\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}
and the space of functors
\mathscr{D}\times_{\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}\widehat{F}\to\widehat{G}
over
\mathscr{B}
:

\mathrm{Map}_{/\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{D},\widehat{S})\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{/\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{D}\times_{\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}\widehat{F},\widehat{G}).

In particular, sections of
\widehat{S}
are equivalently given by functors
\widehat{F}\to\mathrm{Fun}_{/\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{B},\widehat{G})
.

Proof.

Consider the free cocartesian fibration

\mathfrak{F}(H):\mathfrak{F}(\mathscr{D})\to\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}

induced by the functor
H
, where
\mathfrak{F}(\mathscr{D})
is defined by the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{F}(\mathscr{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{source}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}

and
\mathfrak{F}(H)
is the composition

\mathfrak{F}(\mathscr{D})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B})\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{target}}\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}.

This was originally defined for
cartesian
fibrations in
[
GHN17
, Definition 4.1]
, and here we reversed the appropriate source and target maps to obtain a version for cocartesian fibrations. The cocartesian variant of
[
GHN17
, Theorem 4.5]
states that

\mathrm{Map}_{/\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{D},\widehat{S})\simeq\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{cocart}}_{/\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}(\mathfrak{F}(\mathscr{D}),\widehat{S})

where the space on the right is that of maps of cocartesian fibrations. Notice, moreover, that
\mathfrak{F}(H)
classifies the overcategory functor

\mathscr{D}_{/(-)}:=\mathscr{D}\downarrow(-):\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}

defined on objects
(a,b)\in\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}
by the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}_{/(a,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B})_{/(a,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}

This can be seen by examining a fiber of
\mathfrak{F}(H)
: we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}_{(a,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{F}(\mathscr{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B})_{/(a,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{source}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{target}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(a,b)\}}

in which every square is a pullback; this implies that the top horizontal rectangle is a pullback, so in particular
\mathscr{P}_{(a,b)}
is both the fiber of
\mathfrak{F}(H)
over
(a,b)
and the pullback
\mathscr{D}_{/(a,b)}
defined above. Given this information, we can manipulate various end formulas (see
Section

A.3
) to get

\displaystyle\mathrm{Nat}(\mathscr{D}_{/(-)},S(-))

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{(a,b)\in\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}\mathrm{Map}(\mathscr{D}_{/(a,b)},\mathrm{Fun}(F(a),G(b)))

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{b\in\mathscr{B}}\int_{a\in\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Map}((\mathscr{D}_{/(a,b)})\times F(a),G(b))

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{b\in\mathscr{B}}\mathrm{Map}\left(\int^{a\in\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}((\mathscr{D}\times_{\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}})_{/a})\times F(a))_{/b},G(b)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{b\in\mathscr{B}}\mathrm{Map}\left(\left((\mathscr{D}\times_{\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}\left(\int^{a\in\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}F(a)\times(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}})_{/a}\right)\right)_{/b},G(b)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{b\in\mathscr{B}}\mathrm{Map}((\mathscr{D}\times_{\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}\widehat{F})_{/b},G(b))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Nat}((\mathscr{D}\times_{\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}\widehat{F})_{/(-)},G(-))

where the coend formula

\widehat{F}\simeq\int^{a\in\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}F(a)\times(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}})_{/a}=\colim\left(\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\xlongrightarrow{F(-)\times(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}})_{/(-)}}\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}\right)

appears as
[
GHN17
, Corollary 7.6]
with slightly different notation
4
4

4

In
[
GHN17
]
the twisted arrow category
\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{A})
is defined such that
\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathscr{A}\times\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a right fibration, while for us
\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{A}
is a left fibration – see
Section

A.3
. After a careful rearrangement of (co)ends, opposites, and (co)limits we obtain the desired formula.
. Hence we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Map}_{/\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{D},\widehat{S})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{cocart}}_{/\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}(\mathfrak{F}(\mathscr{D}),\widehat{S})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Nat}(\mathscr{D}_{/(-)},S(-))

(straightening)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Nat}((\mathscr{D}\times_{\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}\widehat{F})_{/(-)},G(-))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{cocart}}_{/\mathscr{B}}(\mathfrak{F}(\mathscr{D}\times_{\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}\widehat{F}),\widehat{G})

(unstraightening)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{/\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{D}\times_{\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}\widehat{F},\widehat{G})

(free
\dashv
forgetful).

To prove the last assertion let
H=\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}
. Then
\mathrm{Map}_{/\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B},\widehat{S})
is the space of sections of
\widehat{S}
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Map}_{/\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B},\widehat{S})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{/\mathscr{B}}((\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{B})\times_{\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}}\widehat{F},\widehat{G})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{/\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{B}\times\widehat{F},\widehat{G})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}(\widehat{F},\mathrm{Fun}_{/\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{B},\widehat{G})).\qed

Set

F=\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n}\to\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}\subseteq\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1},\quad G=\mathscr{C}^{\times}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}

Then we can use
Proposition

2.13
and
Proposition

A.14
to construct a section of
\widehat{S}=\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}
from a functor

\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}}\times\widehat{\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})\to\mathrm{Fun}_{/\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}},\widehat{\mathscr{C}^{\times}})

with domain the category of elements of
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}
. This is the product of the categories of elements of
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet}
and
\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet}
:

•

the objects of
\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet}}
are pairs
([n],\varphi:[i]\to[n])
where
\varphi
is inert, and a morphism
([m],\varphi)\to([n],\psi)
is pair consisting of a morphism
\alpha:[n]\to[m]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
and a morphism
\varphi\to\alpha_{\ast}\psi
in
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{m}
;

•

the objects of
\widehat{\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet}}
are pairs
([n],\varphi:[i]\to[n])
where
\varphi
is injective, and a morphism
([m],\varphi)\to([n],\psi)
is pair consisting of a morphism
\beta:[n]\to[m]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
and a morphism
\varphi\to\beta_{\ast}\psi
in
\mathbf{\Theta}^{m}
.

Note now that there are “face” functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces([m],\varphi:[i]\to[m])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[i]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces([m],\varphi:[i]\to[m])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[i]}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces([n],\psi:[j]\to[n])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[j]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces([n],\psi:[j]\to[n])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[j]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathbf{\Theta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha,\mu)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta,\nu)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathbf{\Theta}}(\beta)}

where

f_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\alpha)(r)=\alpha(\psi(r))-\varphi(0),\qquad f_{\mathbf{\Theta}}(\beta)(r)=\max\{s\in[i]\mid\varphi(s)\leq\beta(\psi(r))\}.

It is worth pointing out that, since
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n}\subseteq\mathbf{\Theta}^{n}
, the total space
\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet}}
embeds into
\widehat{\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet}}
and the face functor
f_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
is exactly
f_{\mathbf{\Theta}}
restricted to
\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet}}
, as can be observed by calculating
f_{\mathbf{\Theta}}(\beta)
when
\varphi
and
\psi
are inert morphisms. We decided to keep the constructions separate to highlight the difference that ordinary spans and generalized spans play in the paper.

The functor
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\times(m+n)}\to\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
factors as

\mathbf{\Delta}^{\times(m+n)}\to\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}^{\times(m+n)}\xlongrightarrow{\times}\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}

and so its Grothendieck construction is obtained by taking the product of the individual total spaces:

\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}}\times\widehat{\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}}\cong\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet}}^{\times m}\times\widehat{\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet}}^{\times n}

It follows that we have functors

f^{m,n}:=f_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{\times m}\times f_{\mathbf{\Theta}}^{\times n}:\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}}\times\widehat{\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet,\dotsc,\bullet}}\to\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}.

Composing
f^{m,n}
with a functor
\mathcal{P}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})\to\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}},\widehat{\mathscr{C}^{\times}})
will yield a section
\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
of
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}
. This functor
\mathcal{P}
will be constructed later, so for now we will work in complete generality assuming that we have such a
\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
.

Proposition 2.14
.

There is a cocartesian fibration
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}_{/\!\!/\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}
with the following properties:

(1)

a vertex
(F,\eta)\in\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}_{/\!\!/\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}}
in the fiber over
(\langle t\rangle,[\overline{k}],[\overline{l}])
is a generalized span
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
together with a natural transformation

\eta:F\to\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}(t,\overline{k},\overline{l})

of functors
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
;

(2)

\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}_{/\!\!/\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}}
comes with a functor to
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}
that remembers the underlying generalized span
F
.

Proof.

This follows from the much more general statement in
[
Hau18
, Lemma 6.5]
. Explicitly, given a cocartesian fibration
\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{B}
and a section
s:\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{E}
there is a cocartesian fibration
\mathscr{E}_{/\!\!/s}\to\mathscr{B}
with the universal property that, for any functor
\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{B}
, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}_{/\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{E}_{/\!\!/s})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}_{/\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{D}\times[1]\sqcup_{\mathscr{D}\times\{1\}}\mathscr{B},\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{s\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}_{/\mathscr{B}}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{E})}

is a pullback. After setting
\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{B}
equal to the functor
\Delta^{0}\to\mathscr{B}
picking out a point
b\in\mathscr{B}
the diagram tells us that the vertices of
\mathscr{E}_{/\!\!/s}
over
b
correspond to vertices
e\in\mathscr{E}_{b}
together with an arrow
e\to s(b)
in
\mathscr{E}_{b}
, proving (1). After setting
\mathscr{D}=\mathscr{E}_{/\!\!/s}
we can consider the identity functor
\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{E}_{/\!\!/s}
on the top left corner of the diagram, which corresponds to a functor
(\mathscr{E}_{/\!\!/s}\times[1])\sqcup_{\mathscr{E}_{/\!\!/s}\times\{1\}}\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{E}
on the top right corner; restricting the latter to
\mathscr{E}_{/\!\!/s}\times\{0\}
yields the desired forgetful functor
\mathscr{E}_{/\!\!/s}\to\mathscr{E}
, proving (2). The claim then follows by taking
\mathscr{E}=\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}
,
\mathscr{B}=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}
, and
s=\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
.
∎

2.2.2.
Generalized spans with local systems and cartesian base

Together with the results of the previous section we get a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}_{/\!\!/\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}}

where the diagonal arrows are cocartesian fibrations and the two orthogonal arrows preserve cocartesian morphisms.

Definition 2.15
.

Denote by
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{P})^{m,n}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}
a cocartesian fibration obtained by taking the pullback of the above diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{P})^{m,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}_{/\!\!/\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}}

This contains generalized spans in
\mathscr{C}
with local systems determined by
\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
and with an underlying cartesian span.

One immediate consequence of this definition is that given
F\in\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}_{t,\overline{k},\overline{l}}
, the space of local systems that can be put on
F
(i.e. the fiber of the left vertical map) is

\mathrm{Nat}(F,\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}(t,\overline{k},\overline{l})).

It follows that if
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
share the same underlying cartesian span
F
then
G_{1}\simeq G_{2}
if and only if their local systems are equivalent.

In our applications the section
\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
does not factor through cartesian spans so we can only assume that the base is cartesian – compare this with
[
Hau18
, Lemma 6.4]
. However, in the case
n=0
if we look at the fibers over
\langle 1\rangle\in\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
we recover the constructions of
[
Hau18
, Sections 5, 6]
(see there for the notation):

\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,0}_{1}=\overline{\mathrm{SPAN}}_{m}^{+}(\mathscr{C}),\quad\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,0,\mathrm{cart}}_{1}=\mathrm{SPAN}_{m}^{+}(\mathscr{C}),

and

(\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,0}_{/\!\!/\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}})_{1}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{SPAN}_{m}^{+}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{P})&\text{$\mathcal{P}$ is an $m$-uple category object in $\mathscr{C}$,}\\
\overline{\mathrm{SPAN}}_{m}^{+}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{P})&\text{else}.\end{cases}

When
n\neq 0
is arbitrary we can recover
[
Hau18
, Proposition 6.7]
when
\mathcal{P}
is only partially Segal.

Proposition 2.16
.

Assume
\mathcal{P}
satisfies the Segal condition in the first
m
variables from
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Then so does
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}_{/\!\!/\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}}
– more precisely, any functor classified by the cocartesian fibration
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}_{/\!\!/\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}
satisfies the Segal condition in the first
m
variables from
\mathbf{\Delta}^{{\mathrm{op}}}
and in the
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
variable.

Proof.

To verify the first part of the claim it will be enough to check the Segal condition on the fibers after we fix the remaining
n
variables
[\overline{l}]\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,{\mathrm{op}}}
and the variable
\langle t\rangle\in\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
. But then we can adjoin over the factors
\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}
to obtain

(2.2.1)

(\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}_{/\!\!/\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}})_{t,\overline{\bullet},\overline{l}}\simeq{\mathrm{SPAN}}_{m}^{+}(\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}},\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t});\mathcal{P}(-,\overline{l})_{t})_{\overline{\bullet}}

because
\mathcal{P}(-,[\overline{l}])_{t}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{m,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}\subseteq\widehat{\mathscr{C}^{\times}}
is an
m
-uple category object by assumption and so the section
\sigma_{\mathcal{P}(-,[\overline{l}])_{t}}
factors through the cartesian span fibration by
[
Hau18
, Lemma 6.4]
. The result now follows from
[
Hau18
, Proposition 6.7]
.

From the formula in
Equation

2.2.1
and from the fact that
\mathcal{P}
lands in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})
it is also easy to see that the Segal condition holds for the variable
\langle t\rangle
as well, proving the second part of the claim.
∎

2.3.
Modifying symmetric monoidal category objects

In this section we will concentrate on the case
m=2,n=1
. Our goal is to construct a functor
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}
(which will adjoin over to a functor
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})
) from which to build
\sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
using the results of the previous section. This functor is a modification of a symmetric monoidal category object
Q^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}
which is better suited to serve as a local system for generalized spans, in particular for those indexed by
\mathbf{\Theta}^{\bullet}
.

2.3.1.
The path category as a modifying diagram

We begin by introducing the path category, which will serve as the “shape” that we want part of
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}
to have.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)\in\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{2}
denote the
path
2
-category
of the poset
[0,l]=\{0<1<\dotsb<l\}\in\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
. This has objects the positive integers
0,1,\dotsc,l
and morphism categories

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)}(i,j):=\begin{cases}\{S\subseteq[i,j]\mid i,j\in S\}&i\leq j,\\
\emptyset&i>j,\end{cases}

where
\{S\subseteq[i,j]\mid i,j\in S\}
is a poset under inclusion of subsets, and hence an ordinary category

The usual definition of
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
presents it as a simplicially enriched category – see
[
Lan21
, Definition 1.2.56]
for example – because of its usefulness in defining the simplicial nerve. Just by examining the definition we see that
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(0)
,
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(1)
, and
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(2)
look like

(2.3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 01}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 12}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 01}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 012}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 02}

where the (non-trivial)
2
-morphism in
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(2)
corresponds to the (non-degenerate) inclusion
\{0,2\}\subseteq\{0,1,2\}
. In general, the mapping categories only depend on the difference
d=j-i
:

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)}(i,j)\cong(\Delta^{1})^{\times(d-1)}\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(d)}(0,d).

When passing from
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
to
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l+1)
, one only has to add a new mapping category (up to isomorphism) between
0
and
l+1
, and the other mapping categories will be determined by the old ones.

Consider now the bi-cosimplicial object
(-,-):\mathbf{\Delta}^{2}\to\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{2}
where
([m],[n])
is the
2
-category generated by

objects:

\displaystyle x_{0},\dotsc,x_{m};

1
-morphisms:

\displaystyle f_{i,j}:x_{i}\to x_{i+1},

\displaystyle 0\leq i\leq m-1,0\leq j\leq n;

2
-morphisms:

\displaystyle\alpha_{i,j}:f_{i,j}\to f_{i,j+1},

\displaystyle 0\leq i\leq m-1,0\leq j\leq n-1.

The graph underlying
([m],[n])
looks like
n+1
parallel horizontal chains of
1
-morphisms between
m+1
objects together with
m
parallel vertical chains of
2
-morphisms:

(2.3.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}

\dotsb

\dotsb

\dotsb

Much like
[-]:\mathbf{\Delta}\to\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
is the cosimplicial object representing the nerve functor into simplicial sets,
(-,-)
is the bi-cosimplicial object representing a
2
-categorical nerve into bisimplicial sets:

N:\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{2}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}},\mathbf{S}),\quad N(\mathbf{C})_{m,n}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{2}}(([m],[n]),\mathbf{C}).

An
(m,n)
-simplex of
N(\mathbf{C})
is given by
n+1
horizontal sequences of chains of
m
composable
1
-morphisms bounding
m
vertical sequences of chains of
n
composable
2
-morphisms, all in
\mathbf{C}
.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
\mathfrak{d}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}},\mathbf{S})
denote the functor
[l]\mapsto N(\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l))
. Explicitly,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}[l]_{m,n}

\displaystyle=\{\text{$(m,n)$-simplices of $\mathfrak{d}[l]$}\}

\displaystyle=\{\text{diagrams of shape $([m],[n])$ in $\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)$}\}.

We want to think of
\mathfrak{d}[l]
as an “indexing diagram”, so we introduce the following construction:

Definition 2.19
.

For any
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}
, define

\mathfrak{d}X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C},\quad\mathfrak{d}X_{l}:=\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}}\mathfrak{d}[l]_{\bullet,\bullet}\pitchfork X_{\bullet,\bullet}\in\mathscr{C}.

See
Section

A.3
for a discussion on ends in
\infty
-categories. In particular, note that for any
c\in\mathscr{C}
we have an equivalence

\mathscr{C}(c,\mathfrak{d}X_{l})\simeq\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}}\mathscr{C}(c,\mathfrak{d}[l]_{\bullet,\bullet}\pitchfork X_{\bullet,\bullet})\simeq\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}}\mathscr{C}(\mathfrak{d}[l]_{\bullet,\bullet},\mathscr{S}(c,X_{\bullet,\bullet}))\simeq\mathrm{Nat}(\mathfrak{d}[l],\mathscr{C}(c,X))

between the space of maps
c\to\mathfrak{d}X_{l}
and the space of natural transformations
\mathfrak{d}[l]\to\mathscr{C}(c,X)
. Since
\mathfrak{d}[l]
is valued in sets (finite sets, in fact) the natural transformations correspond to parametrized tuples of maps
c\to X
, meaning that for each
([u],[v])\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}
we have a corresponding tuple of maps
c\to X_{u,v}
whose components correspond to the
(u,v)
-simplices of
\mathfrak{d}[l]
.

This observation can be made more precise. Let
\mathbf{E}:=\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}_{\mathfrak{d}[l]/}
fit into the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}},\mathscr{C})^{\mathrm{op}}_{\mathfrak{d}[l]/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}},\mathscr{C})^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

of ordinary categories, where
y
is the co-Yoneda embedding defined on objects by
y(a):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}}(a,-)
. The objects of
\mathbf{E}
are pairs
((u,v),\sigma)
where
([u],[v])\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{d}[l]_{u,v}
, and there is a morphism
((u,v),\sigma)\to((u^{\prime},v^{\prime}),\sigma^{\prime})
between pairs for each morphism
\varphi:([u],[v])\to([u^{\prime}],[v^{\prime}])
in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}
such that
\varphi^{\ast}(\sigma)=\sigma^{\prime}
. We see from
Corollary

A.9
that

\mathfrak{d}X_{l}\simeq\lim(\mathbf{E}\to\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}\xlongrightarrow{\mathfrak{d}[l]}\mathscr{C})\simeq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(u,v)\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{d}[l]_{u,v}\end{subarray}}X_{u,v}.

It’s hard in general to calculate
\mathfrak{d}X_{l}
from this limit formula but we can simplify things considerably by showing that the limit only depends on the non-degenerate simplices of
\mathfrak{d}[l]
and the face maps. We start with a lemma which helps narrow down the range of simplices to consider:

Lemma 2.20
.

The bisimplicial set
\mathfrak{d}[l]
has non-degenerate
(u,v)
-simplices only in the range
u+v\leq l
. That is, if
u+v>l
then any
(u,v)
-simplex is degenerate.

Proof.

Recall that a
(u,v)
-simplex of
\mathfrak{d}[l]
is a chain as in
Equation

2.3.2
with
u+1
many objects of
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
, i.e. integers
i_{0},\dotsc,i_{u}
between
0
and
l
, and a chain of
v
many
2
-morphisms in the mapping set of any two consecutive objects, i.e. inclusions of subsets of
[0,l]
. If
i,j\in[0,l]
then the longest chain of non-identity arrows in the category
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)}(i,j)
(i.e.
2
-morphisms in
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
) has length exactly
j-i-1
. The assumption that the simplex is non-degenerate implies, in particular, that there isn’t a horizontal string of identity
2
-morphisms that can be collapsed. Hence
v
is bounded above by the sum of the lengths of all the possible chains of
2
-morphisms:

v\leq\sum_{k=0}^{u-1}(i_{k+1}-i_{k}-1)=i_{u}-i_{0}-u.

But
i_{u}-i_{0}\leq l
and thus
u+v\leq u+l-u=l
.
∎

Now let
\mathbf{E}^{\star}\subseteq\mathbf{E}
denote the full subcategory containing the pairs
((u,v),\sigma)
where
\sigma
is a non-degenerate simplex.

Proposition 2.21
.

The inclusion
I:\mathbf{E}^{\star}\to\mathbf{E}
is final, meaning that for any functor
p:\mathbf{E}\to\mathscr{C}
we have an equivalence
\lim p\simeq\lim p\circ I
induced by pre-composition with
I
. In particular

\mathfrak{d}X_{l}\simeq\lim(\mathbf{E}^{\star}\to\mathbf{E}\to\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}\xlongrightarrow{\mathfrak{d}[l]}\mathscr{C})\simeq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}u+v\leq l\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{d}[l]^{\star}_{u,v}\end{subarray}}X_{u,v},

where the superscript
\star
on
\mathfrak{d}[l]
indicates that we are only considering the non-degenerate simplices.

Proof.

The recognition theorem for final functors (dual to
[
Lur09a
, 4.1.3.1]
) states that
I
is final precisely if, for every
x\in\mathbf{E}
, the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{E}^{\star}\times_{\mathbf{E}}\mathbf{E}_{/x}
is weakly constractible. It will be enough to prove that it has a terminal object. The objects of
\mathbf{E}^{\star}\times_{\mathbf{E}}\mathbf{E}_{/x}
are arrows
y\to x
in
\mathbf{E}
with
y\in\mathbf{E}^{\star}
and the morphisms are arrows
y\to y^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{E}
over
x
. In our case
x
corresponds to some specified simplex
\sigma\in\mathfrak{d}[l]_{u,v}
. Then there is a
unique
non-degenerate simplex
\widetilde{\sigma}
and a map
\varphi
in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}
such that
\varphi
is the identity or a degeneracy map (meaning that the two components of
\varphi
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
are surjective) and
\varphi^{\ast}(\widetilde{\sigma})=\sigma
. Let
y
be the object of
\mathbf{E}
corresponding to the simplex
\widetilde{\sigma}
. It is easy to see that
\varphi:y\to x
is a terminal object in
\mathbf{E}^{\star}\times_{\mathbf{E}}\mathbf{E}_{/x}
: if
\psi:y^{\prime}\to x
is another object of
\mathbf{E}^{\star}\times_{\mathbf{E}}\mathbf{E}_{/x}
corresponding to some non-degenerate simplex
\tau
with
\psi^{\ast}(\tau)=\sigma
then
\psi
can be factored as a face map
\psi_{1}
followed by a degeneracy map
\psi_{2}
; but faces of non-degenerate simplices are non-degenerate in
\mathfrak{d}[l]
(as each morphism category in
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
is a poset) so we have that
\psi_{1}^{\ast}(\tau)
is non-degenerate and thus must be
\widetilde{\sigma}
by the uniqueness property of
\widetilde{\sigma}
, meaning that
\psi
factors through
y
.

The condition
u+v\leq l
in the limit follows directly from
Lemma

2.20
.
∎

Note that the morphisms in
\mathbf{E}^{\star}
correspond to face maps so
\mathbf{E}^{\star}
does not contain any degeneracy maps. Using
Proposition

2.21
we can explicitly calculate
\mathfrak{d}X_{l}
for small values of
l
. It is helpful to refer to the drawings in
Equation

2.3.1
when following these computations.

•

\mathfrak{d}[0]
is a point and only has one
(0,0)
-simplex. Hence

(2.3.3)

\mathfrak{d}X_{0}\simeq X_{0,0}.

•

\mathfrak{d}[1]
is a free edge and only has one
(1,0)
-simplex with two
(0,0)
-simplices as faces. Hence

(2.3.4)

\mathfrak{d}X_{1}\simeq\lim(X_{0,0}\leftarrow X_{1,0}\to X_{0,0})\simeq X_{1,0}.

•

\mathfrak{d}[2]
has three
(0,0)
-simplices connected by a commutative triangle of
(1,0)
-simplices (i.e. one
(2,0)
-simplex) with composite
f
, one additional
(1,0)
-simplex
g
, and one
(1,1)
-simplex from
g
to
f
. Hence

(2.3.5)

\mathfrak{d}X_{2}\simeq\lim\left(\hbox to207.76pt{\vbox to56.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 103.87749pt\lower-26.9728pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-103.87749pt}{-19.84087pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -143.74 -27.45)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{45.8063pt}{-13.2381pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 63.38 -18.32)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)\simeq X_{2,0}\times_{X_{1,0}}X_{1,1}

where
s
and
t
are the appropriate source and target maps.

•

For a general
l\geq 3
,
\mathfrak{d}[l]
contains the
(l,0)
-simplex corresponding to the composable chain

(\{0,1\},\{1,2\},\dotsc,\{l-1,l\})

of
1
-morphisms in
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
and the
(1,1)
-simplex corresponding to the inclusion

\{0,l\}\subseteq[0,l]

representing a
2
-morphism in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)}(0,l)
. Since the composition of the
(l,0)
-simplex and the target of the
(1,1)
-simplex coincide we obtain a map

\mathfrak{d}X_{l}\to X_{l,0}\times_{X_{1,0}}X_{1,1}.

In particular we have a projection

(2.3.6)

\xi:\mathfrak{d}X_{l}\to X_{1,1}

onto the second factor.

We end this section with an inductive result describing the structure of
\mathfrak{d}X_{l}
, which is really a convoluted (but useful) reformulation of the statement that
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
is obtainable from copies of
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l-1)
together with a new mapping set isomorphic to
(\Delta^{1})^{\times(l-1)}
.

Lemma 2.22
.

Assume
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}
is a
2
-category object in
\mathscr{C}
. Let
l\geq 3
. Denote by
\wp(l)
the poset of non-empty subsets of
[0,l]
and fix a set
\{c_{0},\dotsc,c_{l}\}
of
l+1
many elements of
\mathscr{C}
. There are two functors

\displaystyle A_{l}:\wp(l)^{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\to\mathscr{C},\quad S\mapsto\mathfrak{d}X_{\left\lvert S\right\rvert-1},

\displaystyle B_{l}:\wp(l)^{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\to\mathscr{C},\quad S\mapsto\prod_{i\in S}c_{i}.

Both restrict to functors
A_{l}^{\prime}
and
B_{l}^{\prime}
defined on
\wp(l)^{\mathrm{op}}\setminus\{[0,l]\}\subseteq\wp(l)^{\mathrm{op}}
. Then the canonical restriction map

\mathrm{Nat}(B_{l},A_{l})\to\mathrm{Nat}(B_{l}^{\prime},A_{l}^{\prime})

has empty or contractible fibers.

Proof.

Note that
\wp(l)^{\mathrm{op}}
is a cone on
\wp(l)^{\mathrm{op}}\setminus\{[0,l]\}
, where
[0,l]
is the initial object. From
Corollary

A.11
we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Nat}(B_{l},A_{l})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Nat}(B_{l}^{\prime},A_{l}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\mathscr{C}\left(\prod_{i\in[0,l]}c_{i},\mathfrak{d}X_{l}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\lim_{S\in\wp(l)^{\mathrm{op}}\setminus\{[0,l]\}}\mathscr{C}\left(\prod_{i\in[0,l]}c_{i},\mathfrak{d}X_{\left\lvert S\right\rvert-1}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

It will be enough to prove that
\zeta
, the bottom map in the diagram, has empty or contractible fibers.

Let
c=\prod_{i\in[0,l]}c_{i}
. From the limit formula in
Proposition

2.21
we know that there are equivalences

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}\left(c,\mathfrak{d}X_{l}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}u+v\leq l\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{d}[l]_{u,v}^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{C}\left(c,X_{u,v}\right),

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}\left(c,\mathfrak{d}X_{\left\lvert S\right\rvert-1}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}u+v\leq\left\lvert S\right\rvert-1\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{d}[\left\lvert S\right\rvert-1]_{u,v}^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{C}\left(c,X_{u,v}\right)

and since
X
satisfies the Segal condition we can simplify these limits as

\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}u+v\leq l\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{d}[l]_{u,v}^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{C}\left(c,X_{u,v}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in\{0,1\}\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{d}[l]_{u,v}^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{C}\left(c,X_{u,v}\right),

\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}u+v\leq\left\lvert S\right\rvert-1\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{d}[\left\lvert S\right\rvert-1]_{u,v}^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{C}\left(c,X_{u,v}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in\{0,1\}\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{d}[\left\lvert S\right\rvert-1]_{u,v}^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{C}\left(c,X_{u,v}\right).

We thus have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(c,\mathfrak{d}X_{l})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\lim_{S\in\wp(l)^{\mathrm{op}}\setminus\{[0,l]\}}\mathscr{C}\left(c,\mathfrak{d}X_{\left\lvert S\right\rvert-1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in\{0,1\}\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{d}[l]_{u,v}^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{C}\left(c,X_{u,v}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\wp(l)^{\mathrm{op}}\setminus\{[0,l]\}\\
u,v\in\{0,1\}\\
\sigma\in\mathfrak{d}[\left\lvert S\right\rvert-1]_{u,v}^{\star}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{C}\left(c,X_{u,v}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

where
\rho
is induced by the restrictions along inclusions
S\subset[0,l]
. It is easy to see that the fibers of
\rho
are empty or contractible by examining the low dimensional (non-degenerate) simplices of
\mathfrak{d}[l]
:

•

the
(0,0)
-simplices are the objects of
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
, namely the integers
i=0,1,\dotsc,l
, and these are obtained from the proper inclusions
S=\{i\}\subseteq[0,l]
;

•

the
(1,0)
-simplices are the
1
-morphisms in
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
, corresponding to subsets
S\subseteq[0,l]
; each
S
is canonically expressed as a pushout of sets with two elements using the ordering on
\{0,1,\dotsc,l\}
, namely

S=\{a_{1},\dotsc,a_{s}\}=\{a_{1},a_{2}\}\cup_{\{a_{2}\}}\{a_{2},a_{3}\}\cup_{\{a_{3}\}}\dotsb\cup_{\{a_{s-1}\}}\{a_{s-1},a_{s}\}

(this is the horizontal composition of
1
-morphisms in
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
) and the latter are obtained from proper inclusions
S=\{a_{j},a_{j+1}\}\subseteq[0,l]
;

•

there are no (non-degenerate)
(0,1)
-simplices since
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
is the nerve of a
2
-category;

•

the
(1,1)
-simplices are the
2
-morphisms in
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
, corresponding to inclusions
S\subseteq T
of subsets of
[0,l]
with the same boundary; these inclusions can be canonically factored as successive single-element inclusions, namely

S\subset S\cup\{b_{1}\}\subset S\cup\{b_{1},b_{2}\}\subset\dotsb\subset S\cup\{b_{1},\dotsc,b_{r}\}=T

(this is the vertical composition of
2
-morphisms in
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{ath}(l)
) and each such inclusion is pulled back from the prototypical single-element inclusion
\{0,2\}\subset\{0,1,2\}
of subsets of
[0,l]
.

Therefore if
f:c\to\mathfrak{d}X_{l}
is a morphism in
\mathscr{C}
then
\rho(f)
completely determines
f
via pullbacks along proper inclusions
S\subset[0,l]
– in fact, the argument shows that it is enough to consider those
S
that have at most
3
elements, justifying our assumption that
l\geq 3
. This shows that
\rho
has empty or contractible fibers and thus so does
\zeta
, concluding the proof.
∎

2.3.2.
The construction of
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}

We will now move on to the construction of the promised section
\sigma
of
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{2,1}
. Assume that we have a symmetric monoidal category object
Q^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}
, i.e.
Q^{\otimes}
satisfies the Segal condition in both variables. In particular there is a representable functor

\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}:\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}(\mathscr{S})),\quad\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(c)_{t,k}:=\mathscr{C}(c,Q^{\otimes}_{t,k})

that lands in symmetric monoidal category objects in
\mathscr{S}
.

First, by
Proposition

A.21
and
Proposition

A.15
we can upgrade
Q^{\otimes}
to a symmetric monoidal
2
-category object
P^{\otimes}=\underline{W(Q^{\otimes})}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}
where the first
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
factor encodes the underlying monoid of
Q^{\otimes}
:

P^{\otimes}_{t,m,n}:=Q^{\otimes}_{tm,n}.

We can then consider the category object
P^{\otimes}_{t,m,\bullet}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}
obtained by fixing the first two entries in
P^{\otimes}
and, following
Definition

A.18
, we can produce a symmetric monoidal
3
-simplicial object
\square^{2}P^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{3,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}
by setting

(\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{t,m,n,p}:=(\square^{2}(P^{\otimes}_{t,m,\bullet}))_{n,p}=(\square^{2}(Q^{\otimes}_{tm,\bullet}))_{n,p}.

Note that
\square^{2}P^{\otimes}
satisfies the Segal condition in all four variables, i.e. it is a symmetric monoidal
3
-category object in
\mathscr{C}
. Define
\mathfrak{d}\square^{2}P^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}
by

(\mathfrak{d}\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{t,l,n}:=\mathfrak{d}((\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{t,\bullet,\bullet,n})_{l}=\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}}\mathfrak{d}[l]_{\bullet,\bullet}\pitchfork(\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{t,\bullet,\bullet,n},

where the expression
(\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{t,\bullet,\bullet,n}
inside the end denotes the
2
-category object obtained from
\square^{2}P^{\otimes}
by fixing the first and last entries and letting the second and third entries vary. Now
\mathfrak{d}\square^{2}P^{\otimes}
only satisfies the Segal condition in the first and last variable – the middle two variables were integrated out and replaced by an extra variable for which the Segal condition does not hold. Finally, let
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}
be given by swapping the second and third factors of
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
and upgrading
\mathfrak{d}\square^{2}P^{\otimes}
to a symmetric monoidal category object in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}},\mathscr{C})
using the constructions of
Proposition

A.21
and
Proposition

A.15
again:

(2.3.7)

\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,l}:=(\mathfrak{d}\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{tk,l,n}.

Proposition 2.23
.

The functor
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}
satisfies the Segal condition in the first three variables, namely the ones from the factors
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}
.

Proof.

Two of the three constructions applied – upgrading a commutative algebra object and passing to commutative squares – preserve the Segal condition by the results in
Section

A.5
. The first three variables in
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}
are obtained from a symmetric monoidal category object
Q^{\otimes}
using exactly those constructions, and so we are done.
∎

In particular we can think of
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}
as a functor
\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})
and thus it defines a section
\sigma_{\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}}
of
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{2,1}
by the discussion following
Proposition

2.13
. We then obtain a cocartesian fibration
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes})^{2,1}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}
as in
Definition

2.15
. The vertices of this fibration are generalized cartesian spans
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{a,c}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{b}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
together with a natural transformation
\eta:F\to\sigma_{\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}}(t,a,c,b)
. The choice of labels
k_{1}=a,k_{2}=c,l_{1}=b
is intentional since we want to think of the
\mathbf{\Theta}
factor as parametrizing the
2
-morphisms, not the
3
-morphisms, of our desired
(\infty,3)
-category.

2.3.3.
A description of
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}

To clarify all the steps in this construction we will compute
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,l}
(for small
t,k,n,l
) in terms of the underlying symmetric monoidal category object
Q:=Q^{\otimes}_{1,\bullet}
. When we speak of an object, morphism, etcetera
of

Q
we always mean
classified by

Q
– that is, the element, morphism, etcetera classified by a map
c\to Q_{\bullet,\bullet}
belongs to the symmetric monoidal category object
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(c)\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}(\mathscr{S}))
.

First note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,0}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}}\mathfrak{d}[0]_{\bullet,\bullet}\pitchfork(\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{kt,\bullet,\bullet,n}\simeq(\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{kt,0,0,n}\simeq Q^{\otimes}_{0,n}\simeq\ast,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}}\mathfrak{d}[1]_{\bullet,\bullet}\pitchfork(\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{kt,\bullet,\bullet,n}\simeq(\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{kt,1,0,n}\simeq Q^{\otimes}_{kt,n},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,2}

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}}\mathfrak{d}[2]_{\bullet,\bullet}\pitchfork(\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{tk,\bullet,\bullet,n}

\displaystyle\simeq(\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{tk,2,0,n}\times_{(\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{tk,1,0,n}}(\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{tk,1,1,n}

\displaystyle\simeq Q^{\otimes}_{2tk,n}\times_{Q^{\otimes}_{tk,n}}(\square^{2}Q^{\otimes})_{tk,1,n},

for any
t,k,n
, by the calculations in
Equations

2.3.3
,
2.3.4
and
2.3.5
.

The monoidal structure
is controlled by the parameter
t
. Since
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,\bullet,\bullet,\bullet}
satisfies the Segal condition in the variable
t
, its values for
t\geq 1
are determined by the value at
t=1
so we will fix
t=1
from now on and only focus on the underlying multi-simplicial object.

Objects
are classified by
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,0,0,0}\simeq\ast
, so there is a contractible choice of objects and their
tensor product
is similarly trivial.

1
-morphisms
are classified by
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,1,0,0}\simeq\ast
, so there is a contractible choice of
1
-morphisms and their
horizontal composition
is similarly trivial.

2
-morphisms
are classified by
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,1,0,1}\simeq Q^{\otimes}_{1,0}=Q_{0}
. Thus the
2
-morphisms are classified by the objects of
Q
. The
horizontal composition of
2
-morphisms
is classified by
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,2,0,1}\simeq Q^{\otimes}_{2,0}
, meaning that it is just given by the tensor product of the objects of
Q
. The
vertical composition of
2
-morphisms
is classified by

\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,1,0,2}\simeq Q^{\otimes}_{2,0}\times_{Q^{\otimes}_{1,0}}Q^{\otimes}_{1,1},

where the pullback is taken along the tensor product map in the first factor and the target map in the second factor (see
Equation

2.3.5
). Thus the vertical composition of two
2
-morphisms, represented by two objects of
Q
, is classified by morphisms into their tensor product:

x,y\text{ in $Q$}\quad\leadsto\quad\text{morphisms }z\to x\otimes y\text{ in $Q$}.

Note that this is a possibly multivalued operation as there might be multiple morphisms into
x\otimes y
.

3
-morphisms
are classified by
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,1,1,1}\simeq Q^{\otimes}_{1,1}=Q_{1}
, so they are given by morphisms in
Q
. The
horizontal composition of
3
-morphisms
is classified by
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,2,1,1}\simeq Q^{\otimes}_{2,1}
, so it is given by the tensor product of morphisms in
Q
. The
vertical composition of
3
-morphisms
is classified by

\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,1,1,2}\simeq Q^{\otimes}_{2,1}\times_{Q^{\otimes}_{1,1}}(\square^{2}Q^{\otimes}_{1,\bullet})_{1,1},

i.e. by commutative squares of morphisms in
Q
one side of which is the tensor product of the morphisms in
Q
representing the chosen
3
-morphisms:

x\xlongrightarrow{f}x^{\prime},y\xlongrightarrow{g}y^{\prime}\text{ in $Q$}\quad\leadsto\quad\text{commutative squares }\hbox to95.71pt{\vbox to49.88pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 47.85295pt\lower-24.93948pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

This composition is easily seen to be compatible with the vertical composition of the
2
-morphisms bounding the chosen
3
-morphisms. The
transversal composition of
3
-morphisms
is classified by
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,1,2,1}\simeq Q^{\otimes}_{1,2}=Q_{2}
and so it is given by the composition of arrows in
Q
, as expected.

The diagram in
Figure

4
is a visual representation of (most of) the data contained in a generalized span
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{l}\to\mathscr{C}_{t}
with local systems valued in
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}
, in the simple case
(t,k,n,l)=(1,1,0,2)
. We believe this is the most instructive case when
l=2
, as can be deduced from the computations above, since varying
k
and
n
only serves to increase the number of such diagrams and varying
t
allows us to pass to tuples of objects and morphisms without much of a change. The picture for
l\geq 3
is more complicated and will be explained in detail shortly. For convenience we also note that the non-preferred kinds of
1
- and
2
-morphisms end up being trivial:

\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,0,1,0},\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,0,0,1},\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,1,1,0},\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{1,0,1,1}\simeq\ast.

Example of a generalized span
F\in\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes})^{0,1}_{1,1,0,2}
. Here each
B_{I}:=\bigcap_{i\in I}B_{i}
is an object of
\mathscr{C}
,
x,y,z
are objects of

, and

is a morphism of

. The underlying cartesian span is in black and the local systems are in red, while the blue shadow is a reminder of the path category

and

pulled back from

, and the morphism
