.

2.4.
Enforcing the push-pull condition

In this section we will construct the symmetric monoidal
(\infty,3)
-category
\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
by first restricting ourselves to a special subcategory of
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes})^{2,1}
, proving that it induces a functor
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
which satisfies the Segal condition in all variables, and then passing to Segal spaces.

From our previous calculations we can see that the problem in obtaining a
3
-category object lies in the vertical composition of
2
-morphisms (and hence also of
3
-morphisms), which is a priori multivalued. In order to fix this issue we will need to make an assumption about the symmetric monoidal category object
Q^{\otimes}
.

Assumption 2.24
.

Consider the representable functor

\mathscr{Q}:\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Seg}^{1}(\mathscr{S})\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1},\qquad\mathscr{Q}(c)_{n}:=\mathscr{C}(c,Q^{\otimes}_{1,n}).

We require that for every
f:c\to d
in
\mathscr{C}
there is an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}(d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Q}(c).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\ast}}

\dashv

The left adjoint
f^{\ast}
will be called the
pullback
by
f
and the right adjoint
f_{\ast}
will be called the
pushforward
by
f
. Moreover, these adjunctions extend to ensure that

\mathscr{Q}^{\dagger}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathrm{Seg}^{1}(\mathscr{S})\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1},\quad\mathscr{Q}^{\dagger}(f)=f_{\ast}

is again a functor of
\infty
-categories.

We need this assumption to be able to talk about the push-pull condition, which we now turn to.

2.4.1.
Push-pull spans, abstractly

Consider
(F,\eta)\in\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes})^{2,1}_{t,k,n,l}
and assume
l\geq 2
. We treat
F
as a functor

F:\mathbf{\Theta}^{l}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n},\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t})

and
\eta:F\to\sigma_{\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}}(t,k,n,l)
as a morphism in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Theta}^{l},\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n},\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}))
. For any
S\in\mathbf{\Theta}^{l}
(i.e.
\emptyset\neq S\subseteq[0,l]
) let
u_{S}=F(S)\in\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
, and recall that

u_{S}\simeq\prod_{i\in S}u_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}

since
F
is cartesian. In particular we set
u=u_{[0,l]}
and we denote by
\pi_{S}:u\to u_{S}
the canonical projections.

By definition of
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}
, the functor
\sigma_{\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}}(t,k,n,l):\mathbf{\Theta}^{l}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n},\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t})
takes the value

\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,\spadesuit,\clubsuit,l}:=\mathfrak{d}((\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{t\cdot\spadesuit,\bullet,\bullet,\clubsuit})_{l}:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}

on the element
S=[0,l]
of
\mathbf{\Theta}^{l}
, where the variables denoted by
\bullet
come from the bisimplicial object that the operator
\mathfrak{d}(-)
is acting on (see
Definition

2.19
) and the variables denoted by
\spadesuit
and
\clubsuit
are the ones coming from the elements
(x,y)
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n}=\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k}\times\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n}
via the “face functors” of
Section

2.2.1
. We call
\spadesuit
and
\clubsuit
the
height
of
x
and
y
, respectively, because informally they correspond to the vertical position of
x
and
y
in the pyramid-like posets
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n}
. All together, we have a local system

\eta:u\to\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,\spadesuit,\clubsuit,l}

which can be thought of as specifying a map
u(x,y)\to\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,K,N,l}
in
\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}
for every element
(x,y)\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n}=\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k}\times\mathbf{\Sigma}^{n}
where
x
and
y
have height
K
and
N
, respectively.

By
Equation

2.3.6
we have a projection

\xi\circ\eta:u\to\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,\spadesuit,\clubsuit,l}=\mathfrak{d}((\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{t\cdot\spadesuit,\bullet,\bullet,\clubsuit})_{l}\to(\square^{2}P^{\otimes})_{t\cdot\spadesuit,1,1,\clubsuit}\simeq(\square^{2}Q^{\otimes}_{t\cdot\spadesuit,\bullet})_{1,\clubsuit}.

The Segal condition guarantees an equivalence

(\square^{2}Q^{\otimes}_{t\cdot\spadesuit,\bullet})_{1,\clubsuit}\simeq(\square^{2}Q^{\otimes}_{1,\bullet})_{1,\clubsuit}^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}=(\square^{2}Q_{\bullet})_{1,\clubsuit}^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}

so the local system
\xi\circ\eta
precisely classifies, for every
(x,y)\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n}
, a tuple (of size enumerated by
t\cdot\spadesuit
) of vertical commutative blocks in
\mathscr{Q}(u(x,y))
. The latter are towers of commutative squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{R}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{R}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{R}_{\clubsuit-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{\clubsuit-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{R}_{\clubsuit}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{\clubsuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}_{\clubsuit-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}_{\clubsuit}}

together with their higher composition and coherence data, where the length of the vertical sides is enumerated by
\clubsuit
. Since the components of the tuples are independent of each other we will instead think of
\xi\circ\eta
as a classifier for one single commutative block valued in the product
\mathscr{Q}(u)^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}
.

Each
\overline{\varphi}_{i}
comes from the basic projection
\xi
described in
Equation

2.3.6
so the sources
\overline{R}_{i}
and targets
\overline{P}_{i}
are constrained by some associated local systems. The sources
\overline{R}_{i}
fit into a diagram
5
5

5

We are abusing notation a little by using the same symbol to refer to a classifying morphism (such as map
a:c\to Q_{1}
in
\mathscr{C}
) and that which it classifies (such as the morphism
a
in
\mathscr{Q}(c)
). We ask forgiveness from the reader for this necessary confusion introduced purely to streamline the notation and avoid getting lost in the indices.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{1}^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{0}\times u_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{0}^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{R}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{source}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r}_{i}}

induced by the local system from the inclusion
\{0,l\}\subseteq[0,l]
and thus

\overline{R}_{i}\simeq\pi_{0,l}^{\ast}(\overline{r}_{i})

in
\mathscr{Q}(u)^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}
.

The targets
\overline{P}_{i}
fit into a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{1}^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\lim_{0\leq j\leq l-1}(u_{j}\times u_{{j+1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q^{\otimes}_{l,0})^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{0}^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim\pi_{j,j+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{target}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{p}_{i,j})_{0\leq j\leq l-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}

induced by the local system from the chain of inclusions
\{j,j+1\}\subseteq[0,l]
and thus

\overline{P}_{i}\simeq\bigotimes_{j=0}^{l-1}\pi_{j,{j+1}}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,j})

in
\mathscr{Q}(u)^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}
; this equivalence is not necessary in our definition of the push-pull condition, but we will need it later. In conclusion we have tuples of morphisms

\overline{\varphi}_{i}:\pi_{0,l}^{\ast}(\overline{r}_{i})\to\overline{P}_{i}

in
\mathscr{Q}(u)^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}
which, thanks to the adjunction
\pi_{0,l}^{\ast}\dashv(\pi_{0,l})_{\ast}
, yield new tuples of morphisms

(2.4.1)

\overline{\varphi}_{i}^{\dagger}:\overline{r}_{i}\to(\pi_{0,l})_{\ast}(\overline{P}_{i})

in
\mathscr{Q}(u_{0}\times u_{l})^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}
. We call
\overline{\varphi}_{i}^{\dagger}
the
push-pull (tuples of) maps determined by
(F,\eta)
.

We can now state the condition precisely.

Definition 2.25
.

Let
Q^{\otimes}
satisfy
Assumption

2.24
. We say that
(F,\eta)\in\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes})^{2,1}_{t,k,n,l}

satisfies the push-pull condition
(or
is push-pull
, for short) if

(1)

l=0
or
l=1
, or

(2)

l\geq 2
and for every map
\iota:[m]\to[l]
the induced push-pull tuples of maps
\iota^{\ast}(\overline{\varphi}_{i}^{\dagger})
determined by
\iota^{\ast}(F,\eta)
are equivalences in
\mathscr{Q}(u_{\iota(0)}\times u_{\iota(m)})^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}
.

Denote by
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes})^{2,1}
the full subcategory spanned by the push-pull spans.

Less opaquely, this condition states that a diagram
F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{l}\to\mathscr{C}_{t}
with local systems
\eta
has push-pull equivalences on every slice
F(x,y):\mathbf{\Theta}^{l}\to\mathscr{C}_{t}
corresponding to an object
(x,y)\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n}
and on every face or degeneracy of that slice. It turns out that we only need to consider the maps
\iota:[m]\to[l]
which are injective, meaning that it is enough to look at the faces of
F(x,y)
. This is because if
\lambda
is surjective then the added push-pull maps in
\lambda^{\ast}F(x,y)
are equivalences already, so if we assume that the condition holds for the injections
\iota
then it will also hold for all maps
\psi
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
since there is a unique factorization
\psi=\iota\circ\lambda
of
\psi
into a surjection followed by an injection.

2.4.2.
Push-pull spans, concretely

Let us work out the details of the push-pull condition for a few small values of
l
. We will leave the parametrized framework for a moment and fix an object
(x,y)\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n}
such that the height of
x
is
\spadesuit=k
and the height of
y
is
\clubsuit=n
.

The first interesting case is
l=2
: the local system

u_{0}\times u_{1}\times u_{2}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,2}\simeq Q^{\otimes}_{2tk,n}\times_{Q^{\otimes}_{tk,n}}(\square^{2}(Q^{\otimes}_{tk,\bullet}))_{1,n}

classifies a commutative block

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,1}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{0,0})\otimes\pi_{1,2}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{0,1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,1}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{1,0})\otimes\pi_{1,2}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{1,1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r}_{n-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,1}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{n-1,0})\otimes\pi_{1,2}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{n-1,1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r}_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,1}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{n,0})\otimes\pi_{1,2}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{n,1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}_{n}}

in
\mathscr{Q}(u_{0}\times u_{1}\times u_{2})^{\times tk}
. If each push-pull map

\overline{\varphi}_{i}^{\dagger}:\overline{r}_{i}\to(\pi_{0,2})_{\ast}(\pi_{0,1}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,0})\otimes\pi_{1,2}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,1}))

is an equivalence then
\overline{r}_{i}
is a specified witness for the vertical composition of
\overline{p}_{i,0}
and
\overline{p}_{i,1}
when these are treated as
2
-morphisms in our desired
(\infty,3)
-category. We encourage the reader to compare this description with
Figure

4
, where
tk=1
and
n=0
.

In fact we have almost produced a proof of this statement:

Lemma 2.26
.

The Segal projection

\rho:\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})_{t,k,n,2}\to\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})_{t,k,n,1}\times_{\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})_{t,k,n,0}}\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})_{t,k,n,1}

is an equivalence.

Proof.

This proof will use the “parametrized” formalism developed above, in the sense that we will work with functors
\mathbf{\Theta}^{l}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n},\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t})
and use
\spadesuit
and
\clubsuit
to denote variables coming from the elements of
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n}
. When we make a choice (for example when we choose an inverse of an equivalence), we are implicitly making one simultaneous choice for all elements
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n}
– this is consistent since there are only finitely many such elements.

We will show that each fiber of
\rho
is contractible by showing that any two vertices of it are connected by an essentially unique path. Let
(F,\eta^{F}),(G,\eta^{G})\in\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})_{t,k,n,2}
live over the same fiber of
\rho
: then they share two
1
-dimensional faces
(E_{0},\eta^{E_{0}}),(E_{1},\eta^{E_{1}})
in
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})_{t,k,n,1}
. It follows that

(1)

The underlying cartesian spans
F
and
G
are equivalent. The two faces
E_{0},E_{1}
determine three
0
-dimensional faces
D_{0},D_{1},D_{2}\in\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})_{t,k,n,0}
– just three cartesian spans without any local systems – which agree for both
F
and
G
. There is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})_{t,k,n,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{2}\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})_{t,k,n,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{2}\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{2,1,\mathrm{cart}}_{t,k,n,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{2,1,\mathrm{cart}}_{t,k,n,2}}
underlying span
underlying
\scriptstyle 0
-faces
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the bottom map is an equivalence by the cartesian condition and the vertical maps take both
(F,\eta^{F})
and
(G,\eta^{G})
to the triple
(D_{0},D_{1},D_{2})
. The cartesian span in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{2,1,\mathrm{cart}}_{t,k,n,2}
induced by
(D_{0},D_{1},D_{2})
is the underlying span of both
F
and
G
, and thus they must agree.

(2)

The local systems
\eta^{F}
and
\eta^{G}
have equivalent
0
-dimensional and
1
-dimensional faces. The first assertion is vacuous since
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,0}\simeq\ast
. By assumption
\eta^{E_{0}}
and
\eta^{E_{1}}
are two out of the three
1
-dimensional faces of both
\eta^{F}
and
\eta^{G}
, and thus must agree by assumption. In particular, the target

\overline{P}_{i}\simeq\pi^{\ast}_{0,1}(\overline{p}_{i,0})\otimes\pi^{\ast}_{1,2}(\overline{p}_{i,1})\in\mathscr{Q}(u_{0}\times u_{2}\times u_{1})^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}

of the push-pull maps is the same for both
\eta^{F}
and
\eta^{G}
, since it is obtained from
\eta^{E_{0}}
and
\eta^{E_{1}}
. The last
1
-dimensional faces are, say,
\theta^{F}
and
\theta^{G}
, and as local systems these determine two chains of morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r^{F}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r^{G}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r^{F}}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r^{G}}_{n}}

in
\mathscr{Q}(u_{0}\times u_{2})^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}
. But since both
\eta^{F}
and
\eta^{G}
are push-pull we have equivalences

\overline{r^{F}}_{i}\xlongrightarrow[\simeq]{\overline{\varphi^{F}}_{i}^{\dagger}}(\pi_{0,2})_{\ast}(\overline{P}_{i})\xlongleftarrow[\simeq]{\overline{\varphi^{G}}_{i}^{\dagger}}\overline{r^{G}}_{i}

in
\mathscr{Q}(u_{0}\times u_{2})^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}
commuting with the vertical maps in the chains, meaning that we can choose essentially unique horizontal equivalences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r^{F}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r^{G}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r^{F}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r^{G}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r^{F}}_{\clubsuit-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r^{G}}_{\clubsuit-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r^{F}}_{\clubsuit}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r^{G}}_{\clubsuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\clubsuit-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\clubsuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

in
\mathscr{Q}(u_{0}\times u_{2})^{\times t\cdot\spadesuit}
commuting with the push-pull maps and making each square commute. Hence
\theta^{F}\simeq\theta^{G}
.

(3)

The local systems
\eta^{F}
and
\eta^{G}
are equivalent on their only
2
-dimensional face. These local systems are determined by the three
1
-dimensional faces
\eta^{E_{0}},\eta^{E_{1}},\theta^{F}\simeq\theta^{G}
shared by
\eta^{F}
and
\eta^{G}
and the two commutative blocks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{F}}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{G}}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{F}}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{G}}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{F}}_{\clubsuit-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{\clubsuit-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{G}}_{\clubsuit-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{F}}_{\clubsuit})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{\clubsuit}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{G}}_{\clubsuit})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{F}}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{G}}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{F}}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{G}}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{F}}_{\clubsuit-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{G}}_{\clubsuit-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{F}}_{\clubsuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{G}}_{\clubsuit}}

which they classify. But by the previous point this large commutative block can be extended to a commutative triangular prism whose front side consists entirely of equivalences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{F}}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{G}}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{F}}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{G}}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{F}}_{\clubsuit-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{G}}_{\clubsuit-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{\clubsuit-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{F}}_{\clubsuit})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{r^{G}}_{\clubsuit})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{P}_{\clubsuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\psi_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{F}}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{G}}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\psi_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{F}}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{G}}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\psi_{\clubsuit-1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{F}}_{\clubsuit-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{G}}_{\clubsuit-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\psi_{\clubsuit})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{F}}_{\clubsuit}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi^{G}}_{\clubsuit}}

Since the triangular slices and the square faces are all commutative, and since the horizontal maps are equivalences, the two back sides are equivalent commutative blocks. Hence
\eta^{F}\simeq\eta^{G}
, as needed.

All of the choices made in establishing the equivalence between
\eta^{F}
and
\eta^{G}
are essentially unique, since the
\psi_{i}
’s were chosen from a contractible space of inverses all at once for every element of
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n}
, and compatible, since we made them all at once for all possible values of
\spadesuit
and
\clubsuit
.
∎

Going back for one last example (where again we fix
(x,y)\in\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n}
with
\spadesuit=k
and
\clubsuit=n
), when
l=3
we have six local systems

\displaystyle u_{0}\times u_{1}\xlongrightarrow{\overline{p_{\bullet,0}}}\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,1},

\displaystyle u_{1}\times u_{2}\xlongrightarrow{\overline{p_{\bullet,1}}}\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,1},

\displaystyle u_{2}\times u_{3}\xlongrightarrow{\overline{p_{\bullet,2}}}\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,1}

\displaystyle u_{0}\times u_{2}\xlongrightarrow{\overline{q_{\bullet}}}\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,1},

\displaystyle u_{1}\times u_{3}\xlongrightarrow{\overline{r_{\bullet}}}\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,1},

\displaystyle u_{0}\times u_{3}\xlongrightarrow{\overline{s_{\bullet}}}\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,1}

on the
1
-dimensional faces of
F
which classify six chains

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{0,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{0,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{0,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{q}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{s}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{1,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{1,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{1,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{q}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{s}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{n-1,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{n-1,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{n-1,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{q}_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r}_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{s}_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{n,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{n,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{n,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{q}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{s}_{n}}

of
tk
-tuples of
1
-morphisms their respective
\infty
-categories
\mathscr{Q}(-)
. The four local systems

\displaystyle u_{0}\times u_{1}\times u_{2}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,2},

\displaystyle u_{1}\times u_{2}\times u_{3}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,2}

\displaystyle u_{0}\times u_{1}\times u_{3}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,2},

\displaystyle u_{0}\times u_{2}\times u_{3}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,2}

on the
2
-dimensional faces of
F
determine equivalences

\displaystyle\overline{q}_{i}\simeq(\pi_{0,2})_{\ast}(\pi_{0,1}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,0})\otimes\pi_{1,2}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,1})),

\displaystyle\overline{r}_{i}\simeq(\pi_{1,3})_{\ast}(\pi_{1,2}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,1})\otimes\pi_{2,3}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,2})),

\displaystyle\overline{s}_{i}\simeq(\pi_{0,3})_{\ast}(\pi_{0,1}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,0})\otimes\pi_{1,3}^{\ast}(\overline{r}_{i})),

\displaystyle\overline{s}_{i}\simeq(\pi_{0,3})_{\ast}(\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{q}_{i})\otimes\pi_{2,3}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,2}))

via the push-pull maps. Finally, the local system

u_{0}\times u_{1}\times u_{2}\times u_{3}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,k,n,3}

on the only

-dimensional face of

ensures that these equivalences fit into the commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{s}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{0,3})_{\ast}(\pi_{0,1}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,0})\otimes\pi_{1,3}^{\ast}(\overline{r}_{i}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{0,3})_{\ast}(\pi_{0,2}^{\ast}(\overline{q}_{i})\otimes\pi_{2,3}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{0,3})_{\ast}(\pi_{0,1}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,0})\otimes\pi_{1,2}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,1})\otimes\pi_{2,3}^{\ast}(\overline{p}_{i,2}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

of

-tuples in
\mathscr{Q}(u_{0}\times u_{3})
. Thus
\overline{s}_{i}
is a specified witness for the vertical composition of
\overline{p}_{i,0}
,
\overline{p}_{i,1}
, and
\overline{p}_{i,2}
as
2
-morphisms, and also contains the data of all possible choices of bracketing.

2.4.3.
The
(\infty,3)
-category of push-pull spans

Our next order of business is to show that
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
determines a symmetric monoidal
(\infty,3)
-category. First we show that it determines a functor from
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}
.

Proposition 2.27
.

The projection
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes})^{2,1}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}
is a cocartesian fibration.

Proof.

Since the inclusion
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes})^{2,1}
is full it will be enough to prove that the action of
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
in
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1}
on
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
is closed, i.e. that for any
(F,\eta)\in\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})_{t,k,n,l}
the new span
(\alpha_{\ast},\beta^{\ast},\gamma^{\ast},\delta^{\ast})(F,\eta)
is in
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
. Acting by
\alpha
and
\beta
only changes the number of components in each push-pull tuple by either adding identity morphisms (which are trivially push-pull), composing some morphisms (which preserves equivalences), or forgetting some morphisms; these operations evidently preserve the push-pull condition. Acting by
\gamma
changes the height of the commutative blocks by possibly adding identities, composing, or forgetting morphisms, but this does not affect the push-pull maps themselves. Acting by
\delta
preserves the push-pull condition by induction. Suppose
\delta
goes from
[m]
to
[l]
, assume
(F,\eta)
is push-pull, and let
\varepsilon:[h]\to[m]
be another map in
\mathbf{\Delta}
. Then the push-pull maps in
(\delta\circ\varepsilon)^{\ast}(F,\eta)
are equivalences by
Definition

2.25
, and since

\varepsilon^{\ast}(\delta^{\ast}(F,\eta))\simeq(\delta\circ\varepsilon)^{\ast}(F,\eta)

we see that the push-pull maps in
\varepsilon^{\ast}(\delta^{\ast}(F,\eta))
are also equivalences. But
\varepsilon
was arbitrary, so it follows
\delta^{\ast}(F,\eta)
is push-pull, as required.
∎

Definition 2.28
.

Denote by
\mathrm{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes}):\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
a functor obtained by straightening the cocartesian fibration
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})

Proposition 2.29
.

The functor
\mathrm{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
satisfies the Segal condition in the first three variables.

Proof.

We simplify the notation by letting
\mathscr{X}=\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
and
\mathscr{Y}=\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C};\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes})^{2,1}
. The inclusion
X\subseteq Y
is compatible with the Segal maps: for every quadruple of indices
(t,k,n,l)
we have pullback diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}_{t,k,n,l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}_{t,k,n,l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}_{b}\times_{\mathscr{X}_{c}}\dotsb\times_{\mathscr{X}_{c}}\mathscr{X}_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}_{b}\times_{\mathscr{Y}_{c}}\dotsb\times_{\mathscr{Y}_{c}}\mathscr{Y}_{b}}

one for each row in the matrix with columns
(b,c)
given by

b=\begin{pmatrix}(1,k,n,l)\\
(t,1,n,l)\\
(t,k,1,l)\\
(t,k,n,1)\end{pmatrix},\quad c=\begin{pmatrix}(0,k,n,l)\\
(t,0,n,l)\\
(t,k,0,l)\\
(t,k,n,0)\end{pmatrix}.

By
Proposition

2.23
the functor
\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}
satisfies the Segal condition in the first three variables
(\langle t\rangle,[k],[n])\in\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}
and thus by
Proposition

2.16
(applied to the induced functor
\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})
) the right vertical maps in the first three diagrams are equivalences. Since the diagrams are pullbacks this implies that the left vertical maps are equivalences and so
\mathrm{PP}(\mathscr{C},Q^{\otimes})
satisfies the Segal condition in the first three variables.
∎

Proposition 2.30
.

The functor
\mathrm{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
satisfies the Segal condition in the last variable.

Proof.

We again let
\mathscr{X}=\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
and we fix a tuple
d=(t,k,n)
of indices. We have Segal face maps

\rho_{i}:\mathscr{X}_{d,l}\to\mathscr{X}_{d,1},

one for each inert inclusion
\{i,i+1\}\subseteq[0,l]
, which assemble to

\rho:\mathscr{X}_{d,l}\to\mathscr{X}_{d,1}\times_{\mathscr{X}_{d,0}}\dotsb\times_{\mathscr{X}_{d,0}}\mathscr{X}_{d,1}

and we want to show that
\rho
is an equivalence. We will prove the following, which readily implies that
\rho
is an equivalence since each of its fibers is non-empty: if
(F,\eta^{F})
and
(G,\eta^{G})
are in the same fiber of
\rho
then there is an essentially unique equivalence
(F,\eta^{F})\simeq(G,\eta^{G})
. The cases
l=0
and
l=1
are trivial and the case
l=2
is proved in
Lemma

2.26
, so by induction we can assume that it holds up to
l-1
. By assumption
F
and
G
agree on their spine, namely the collection of
1
-dimensional faces indexed by the inert inclusions
\{i,i+1\}\subseteq[0,l]
, so by the inductive hypothesis they must agree on all of their
m
-dimensional faces for
m\leq l-1
. This means that
F\simeq G
and thus
(F,\eta^{F})
and
(G,\eta^{G})
have a common underlying cartesian span with (parametrized) vertices
u_{0},\dotsc,u_{l}
and common equivalent local systems

\eta_{S}^{F}\simeq\eta^{G}_{S}:u_{S}\simeq\prod_{i\in S}u_{i}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,\spadesuit,\clubsuit,\left\lvert S\right\rvert-1}

for all proper subsets
S\subset[0,l]
. Since
l\geq 3
we can now apply the setup of
Lemma

2.22
: we have two natural transformation
\eta^{F}
and
\eta^{G}
of functors
A_{l},B_{l}:\wp(l)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k,n},\mathscr{C}_{t}^{\times})
which are equivalent upon restricting to
\wp(l)^{\mathrm{op}}\setminus\{[0,l]\}
. The lemma implies that the common restriction has an essentially unique extension to
\wp(l)^{\mathrm{op}}
, which readily implies
\eta^{F}\simeq\eta^{G}
via an essentially unique equivalence. This concludes the proof.
∎

Theorem 2.31
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an
\infty
-category with finite limits and let
Q:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}
be a symmetric monoidal category object in
\mathscr{C}
satisfying
Assumption

2.24
. The functor
\mathrm{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes}):\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{3,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
determines a symmetric monoidal
(\infty,3)
-category.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.29
and
Proposition

2.30

\mathrm{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
satisfies the Segal condition, so it defines an object of
\mathrm{Cat}^{3}(\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}))\simeq\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}^{3}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}))
. Passing first to
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}^{3}(\mathscr{S}))
via the functor
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}\to\mathscr{S}
which takes the maximal subspace of an
\infty
-category (i.e. keeping all and only the invertible morphisms) and then passing to complete Segal spaces via
Proposition

A.6
gives an object of
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{3})
.
∎

Definition 2.32
.

We denote by
\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{3})
a symmetric monoidal
(\infty,3)
-category obtained in
Theorem

2.31
and we call it the
3
-category of push-pull spans in
\mathscr{C}
valued in
Q^{\otimes}
.

By construction, the
(\infty,3)
-category
\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
has the property that the
3
-morphisms between two
2
-morphisms
P,Q\in\mathscr{Q}(Z_{1}\cap Z_{2})
are given by a pair
(W,\alpha)
, where
W\in\mathscr{C}_{/Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}}
comes with a map
i:W\to Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}
and
\alpha:i^{\ast}P\to i^{\ast}Q
is a map in
\mathscr{Q}(W)
. It is straightforward to pass to the subcategory where all such
W
are equivalent to
Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}
(i.e. where
i
is an equivalence), thus recovering exactly the desired property (c’). We do this explicitly in
Section

3.2
, but doing so now recovers exactly
Theorem

A
.

One note must be made about completeness here. Recall the functors
R^{3}
and
L^{3}
from
Proposition

A.6
. If
\mathscr{C}
is an
\infty
-topos and
Q^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{{\mathrm{op}}},\mathscr{C})
is a symmetric monoidal
complete
Segal object in
\mathscr{C}
(see
[
Hau18
, Definition 7.4]
) then using similar arguments to those in
[
Hau18
, Section 9]
one can prove that
R^{3}(\mathrm{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes}))\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Seg}^{3}(\mathscr{S}))
is already complete, in which case applying
L^{3}
does noting. In any case, though, completing an
n
-fold Segal space does not affect the dualizability of its
k
-morphisms, so for the sake of computations it is good enough to work with the
3
-fold Segal space underlying
\mathrm{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
. This is (implicitly) what we will do in
Section

3.2
.

Since the data of
Q^{\otimes}
is equivalent to that of the functor
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}:\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Cat}(\mathscr{S}))
which it represents, we may sometimes say that the local systems are valued in or determined by
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}(-)
. This terminology is more convenient for the section to come.

2.4.4.
Relative push-pull spans

The construction of
\mathscr{PP}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
requires that
Q^{\otimes}
be a (parametrized) object of
\mathscr{C}
satisfying some nice properties associated to the functor which it represents. Say
\mathscr{D}^{\odot}
is a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category (not necessarily cartesian monoidal) that admits a symmetric monoidal functor
U:\mathscr{D}^{\odot}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}
to
\mathscr{C}
with its cartesian monoidal structure.

Definition 2.33
.

We let

\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D}^{\odot})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D}^{\odot})^{m,n}

denote the full subcategory spanned by the
generalized
\odot
-cartesian spans in
\mathscr{D}
, namely those spans

F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{D}^{\odot}_{t}

such that the composite

U_{t}\circ F:\mathbf{\Sigma}^{\overline{k}}\times\mathbf{\Theta}^{\overline{l}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}

is a cartesian span in
\mathscr{C}
. That is,
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D}^{\odot})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}
fits into the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D}^{\odot})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D}^{\odot})^{m,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n,\mathrm{cart}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{m,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\ast}}

of cocartesian fibrations over
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{m+n,{\mathrm{op}}}
.

It is possible that
Q^{\otimes}
does not lift to
\mathscr{D}
and so, strictly speaking, we cannot talk about
Q^{\otimes}
-valued local systems for spans in
\mathscr{D}
. However, we can do the next best thing: first pass to
\mathscr{C}
via
U_{\ast}
and then ask for local systems on the image, so as to get local systems on spans in
\mathscr{D}
determined by
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}\circ U^{\mathrm{op}}
. More precisely, consider the following diagram of cocartesian fibrations, where the vertical map on the right picks out the underlying generalized cartesian span of a push-pull span in
\mathscr{C}
:

(2.4.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{D}^{\odot})^{2,1,\mathrm{cart}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{C})^{2,1,\mathrm{cart}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\ast}}

Definition 2.34
.

Let
\mathscr{D}^{\odot}
be a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category and let
U:\mathscr{D}^{\odot}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}
be a symmetric monoidal functor. Denote by
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\otimes})\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}
a cocartesian fibration obtained by taking a pullback of the diagram in
Equation

2.4.2
and let
\mathrm{PP}(U;Q^{\otimes}):\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{2+1,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
be a straightening of it.

A vertex of
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\otimes})
is a generalized
\odot
-cartesian span
F
in
\mathscr{D}
together with a push-pull local system on
U_{\ast}(F)
valued in
Q^{\otimes}
– equivalently, a push-pull local system on
F
valued in the
\infty
-categories determined by
\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}\circ U^{\mathrm{op}}(-)
.

Proposition 2.35
.

The functor
\mathrm{PP}(U;Q^{\otimes})
satisfies the Segal condition in all variables.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{X}=\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\otimes})
and
\mathscr{Y}=\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
. Then the Segal maps for
\mathscr{X}
are obtained from those for
\mathscr{Y}
via
U_{\ast}
: for every quadruple of indices
(t,k,n,l)
we have pullback diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}_{t,k,n,l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}_{t,k,n,l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}_{b}\times_{\mathscr{X}_{c}}\dotsb\times_{\mathscr{X}_{c}}\mathscr{X}_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}_{b}\times_{\mathscr{Y}_{c}}\dotsb\times_{\mathscr{Y}_{c}}\mathscr{Y}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\ast}}

one for each row in the matrix with columns
(b,c)
given by

b=\begin{pmatrix}(1,k,n,l)\\
(t,1,n,l)\\
(t,k,1,l)\\
(t,k,n,1)\end{pmatrix},\quad c=\begin{pmatrix}(0,k,n,l)\\
(t,0,n,l)\\
(t,k,0,l)\\
(t,k,n,0)\end{pmatrix}.

That these squares are pullback squares follows immediately from the fact that
U_{\ast}
preserves cartesian spans and from the definition of
\mathscr{X}
. By
Proposition

2.29
and
Proposition

2.30
the right vertical map in all four cases is an equivalence, so the left vertical map is an equivalence as well and we are done.
∎

Definition 2.36
.

The symmetric monoidal
(\infty,3)
-category induced by
\mathrm{PP}(U;Q^{\otimes})
is denoted
\mathscr{PP}(U;Q^{\otimes})
and contains the
U
-relative push-pull spans in
\mathscr{D}
valued in
Q^{\otimes}
, or just
relative push-pull spans
for short.

3.
Applications of push-pull spans

We just showed that given a symmetric monoidal functor
U:\mathscr{D}^{\odot}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}
, where
\mathscr{C}
is cartesian monoidal, and any symmetric monoidal category object
Q:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}
satisfying a certain assumption there is a symmetric monoidal
(\infty,3)
-category
\mathscr{PP}(U:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C};Q^{\otimes})
of spans in
\mathscr{D}
with a push-pull system in
\mathscr{C}
valued in
Q^{\otimes}
. Following in the footsteps of
[
CHS22
]
we will use derived algebraic geometry to define suitable objects that we can plug into our general construction. We will then restrict it a little further to obtain the
(\infty,3)
-category
\mathscr{CRW}
and compare it to Kapustin and Rozansky’s
\mathbf{RW}
from
[
KR10
]
.

It’s worth noting that derived algebraic geometry is not the only setting suitable for Rozansky-Witten field theories. A different and maybe more natural choice would be derived differential geometry, which has derived smooth manifolds (see
[
Spi10
,
Ste23
]
) as its main objects of study. We feel that derived algebraic geometry works better for this paper in particular for several key reasons:

(1)

it allows us to compare our results to those of
[
CHS22
]
(because of the explicit use of derived algebro-geometric methods) and
[
BCR23
,
BCFR23
]
(because matrix factorizations are inherently algebraic, and thus it’s easier to compute field theories with algebraic rather than smooth stacks);

(2)

the universal properties of derived stacks are easier to apply to the construction of
Section

2
, in particular the passage from a limit-preserving functor into symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories to its representing
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
-object which is the key to the arguments in
Sections

2.3
and
2.4
;

(3)

we
6
6

6

The author.
are overall more comfortable with algebraic stacks than with smooth stacks (for educational reasons more than anything else).

However, we want to stress that
Theorem

2.31
is general enough that it can be applied to derived smooth stacks, given a well-behaved choice of
Q^{\otimes}
– the representing object determining the
2
- and
3
-morphisms. We leave the task of producing such a
Q^{\otimes}
to future work.

3.1.
Background on derived algebraic geometry

Here we recall a number of definitions and results from derived algebraic geometry, just enough for our purposes. We refer the reader to
[
TV08
,
PTVV13
]
for a detailed exposition of this theory and the symplectic structures definable in it. We will forgo citing the primary sources directly and instead use the convenient Appendix B of
[
CHS22
]
since the great majority of the material we will need is already covered in that paper.

3.1.1.
Derived stacks

Fix a base field
\mathbb{K}
of characteristic zero. Then there is an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathbb{K}}
of connective
\mathbb{K}
-modules (= cochain complexes of vector spaces over
\mathbb{K}
localized at the quasi-isomorphisms, with vanishing positive cohomology) which is symmetric monoidal with respect to the tensor product of modules (= derived tensor product of cochain complexes). Let
\mathscr{CA}\mathrm{lg}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\mathrm{c}}:=\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathbb{K}})
denote the
\infty
-category of connective
\mathbb{K}
-algebras (= commutative algebras within connective
\mathbb{K}
-modules) – see
[
CHS22
, Definition B.1.1]
. Define
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}:=(\mathscr{CA}\mathrm{lg}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\mathrm{c}})^{\mathrm{op}}
. For any very large presentable
\infty
-category
\mathscr{D}
there is a full subcategory
{\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}}(\mathscr{D})\subseteq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{D})
of presheaves on
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}
with the following properties:

(a)

[
CHS22
, first part of Lemma B.3.7]

\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}:=\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}(\overline{\mathscr{S}})
is an
\infty
-topos (see
[
Lur09a
]
), where
\overline{\mathscr{S}}
is a very large
\infty
-category of large spaces;

(b)

[
CHS22
, Corollary B.3.5]
the Yoneda embedding

\Spec:\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{S}),\quad A\mapsto\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}(-,A)\simeq\mathscr{CA}\mathrm{lg}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\mathrm{c}}(A,-)

factors through
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
, so we can view it as a fully faithful functor
\Spec:\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}\to\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
;

(c)

[
CHS22
, second part of Lemma B.3.7]

\Spec^{\mathrm{op}}
induces a restriction functor
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{D})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{D})
which specializes to an equivalence

\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{R}}(\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{D})\xlongrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}(\mathscr{D})

where the ”
\mathrm{R}
” superscript denotes the full subcategory spanned by those functors which preserve (large) limits; the inverse of this equivalence is given by right Kan extension along
\Spec^{\mathrm{op}}
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
is that of
derived stacks
, a far reaching generalization of schemes and stacks in algebraic geometry, and its full subcategory
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}
is that of
derived affine stacks
(
[
CHS22
, Definition B.3.6]
). We will denote an element of
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}
by
A
if we are thinking of it as an algebra (in its opposite category) and by
\Spec A
if we are thinking of it as a functor
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\overline{\mathscr{S}}
. By (a)
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
is complete, cocomplete, and cartesian closed, and admits an embedding

(-)_{\mathcal{B}}:\mathscr{S}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t},\quad X_{\mathcal{B}}:=\colim_{X}(\Spec\mathbb{K})

called the
Betti stack
(
[
CHS22
, Definition B.3.9]
). By (c) there is an equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{R}}(\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Spec^{\mathrm{op}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

whose inverse is given by right Kan extending along
\Spec^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be an
\infty
-categories enriched over large spaces, let
\mathscr{E}\subseteq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})
be a full subcategory, and let
f:\mathscr{D}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{E}
be the inclusion of a dense full subcategory. Assume further that the restriction
f^{{\mathrm{op}},\ast}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})
gives an equivalence

\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{R}}(\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})\simeq\mathscr{E},

where
\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{R}}
denotes the limit-preserving functors, whose inverse is right Kan extension along
f^{\mathrm{op}}:\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Then, for any limit-preserving
F:\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\overline{\mathscr{S}}
, the element
f^{{\mathrm{op}},\ast}(F)\in\mathscr{E}
is a representing object for
F
:

F(X)\simeq\mathscr{E}(X,f^{{\mathrm{op}},\ast}(F))

naturally in
X\in\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Proof.

Denseness of
f
implies that the Yoneda embedding
y:\mathscr{D}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})
factors through
\mathscr{E}
. First we will show that for any
X\in\mathscr{E}
we have a natural equivalence

X\simeq\colim_{Z\in\mathscr{D}_{/X}}y(Z).

Indeed, the limit-preserving functor
\mathscr{E}(-,Y):\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\overline{\mathscr{S}}
is right Kan extended from
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})
so we have natural equivalences

\mathscr{E}(X,Y)\simeq\lim_{Z\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}_{X/}}\mathscr{E}(f^{\mathrm{op}}(Z),Y)\simeq\mathscr{E}\left(\colim_{Z\in\mathscr{D}_{/X}}f(Z),Y\right)

for any
X\in\mathscr{E}
, and so the Yoneda lemma yields

X\simeq\colim_{Z\in\mathscr{D}_{/X}}f(Z)\simeq\colim_{Z\in\mathscr{D}_{/X}}y(Z)

since
f
and
y
agree on objects. Remember that if
X
is considered as an object of
\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{op}}
then

X\simeq\lim_{Z\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}_{X/}}f^{\mathrm{op}}(Z)

and so, after a few careful applications of the Yoneda lemma,

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}(X,f^{{\mathrm{op}},\ast}(F))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{E}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})}(\mathrm{RKan}_{f^{\mathrm{op}}}(X),F)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})}\left(\mathrm{RKan}_{f^{\mathrm{op}}}\left(\lim_{Z\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}_{X/}}f^{\mathrm{op}}(Z)\right),F\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{Z\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}_{X/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})}(\mathrm{RKan}_{f^{\mathrm{op}}}(f^{\mathrm{op}}(Z)),F)

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{Z\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}_{X/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})}(\mathrm{RKan}_{f^{\mathrm{op}}}(\mathscr{D}(-,Z)),F)

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{Z\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}_{X/}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})}(\mathscr{E}(-,f^{\mathrm{op}}(Z)),F)

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{Z\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}_{X/}}F(f^{\mathrm{op}}(Z))

\displaystyle\simeq F\left(\lim_{Z\in\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}_{X/}}f^{\mathrm{op}}(Z)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq F(X).\qed

Thanks to the lemma we can see that every derived stack is canonically the colimit of the derived affine stacks that map into it:

(3.1.1)

X\simeq\colim_{A\in\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}_{/X}}\Spec(A).

We are more interested in these abstract properties than in the actual implementation of derived stacks, but the interested reader should know that we can take
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
to be the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{CA}\mathrm{lg}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\mathrm{c}},\overline{\mathscr{S}})
spanned by the
étale sheaves
, those product-preserving functors
F
such that, for any étale map
f:A\to B
in
\mathscr{CA}\mathrm{lg}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\mathrm{c}}
,
F(A)
is realized as a limit of the cosimplicial diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(B\otimes_{A}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(B\otimes_{A}B\otimes_{A}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}

induced by
f
(see
[
CHS22
, Definition B.3.1]
). Thus we can think of a derived stack as a presheaf on
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}
which preserves products and satisfies an appropriate notion of descent, which in virtue of the consequences of (c) is given as a canonical colimit of affine presheaves. Given an
X\in\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
we can relativize
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}(\mathscr{D})
to
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}_{X}(\mathscr{D})\subseteq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}^{\mathrm{op}}_{/X},\mathscr{D})
and obtain an equivalence

(3.1.2)

\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{R}}(\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{op}}_{/X},\mathscr{D})\simeq\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}_{X}(\mathscr{D})

(see
[
CHS22
, Lemma B.3.7]
).

3.1.2.
Quasicoherent sheaves

Now let
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}:\mathscr{CA}\mathrm{lg}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\mathrm{c}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1})
denote the functor sending an algebra
A\in\mathscr{CA}\mathrm{lg}_{\mathbb{K}}^{\mathrm{c}}
to the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{A}
of
A
-modules in
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathbb{K}}
. This satisfies a very strong form of descent that characterizes it as an element of
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}))
, and so by condition (c) (or
[
CHS22
, second part of Lemma B.3.7]
) we obtain a limit-preserving functor
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\otimes}:\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1})
(see
[
CHS22
, Definition B.5.2]
)
7
7

7

The target of
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}
should actually be
\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathrm{L}}
, the very large
\infty
-category of
presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories
. Since this is itself presentable we can apply (c) with
\mathscr{D}=\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathrm{L}}
and the argument will follow through. Note, however, that by
[
CHS22
, Definition B.5.12]
the passage to
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1})
is justified and does not significantly affect the functor
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\otimes}
that we obtain in the end.
. From
Equation

3.1.1
we see that

\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\otimes}(X)\simeq\lim_{A\in\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}_{/X}}\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\otimes}(\Spec(A))\simeq\lim_{\Spec A\in\mathrm{d}\mathscr{A}\mathrm{ff}_{/X}}\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{A}.

Define
\mathcal{O}_{X}\in\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\otimes}(X)
to be the element of the limit obtained by taking the algebra
A\in\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{A}
for every
\Spec A\to X
. Then from
[
CHS22
, Proposition B.5.3]
we have that
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\otimes}(X)
corresponds to the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category of
quasicoherent sheaves of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules on
X
. Locally over an affine stack
\Spec A
this is the
\infty
-category of
A
-modules, and in particular the sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}
serves as the unit for the tensor product. For each morphism
f:X\to Y
of derived stacks the corresponding functor
f^{\ast}:\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\otimes}(Y)\to\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\otimes}(X)
is the
pullback
of sheaves; if
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}(X)
denotes the underlying
\infty
-category of
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\otimes}(X)
then
f^{\ast}
has a right adjoint
f_{\ast}:\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}(X)\to\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}(Y)
, the
pushforward
of sheaves. After composing with the inclusion
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1})\subseteq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{S})
and adjoining the two factors we obtain a functor

Q^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{R}}(\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{S})\simeq\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}

which satisfies the Segal condition in both variables and such that

\mathscr{Q}^{\otimes}_{t,n}:=\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\otimes}(X)_{t,n}\simeq\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}(X,Q^{\otimes}_{t,n})

by
Lemma

3.1
; i.e.,
Q^{\otimes}
parametrizes a representable object for the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category of quasicoherent sheaves over a derived stack. In particular, since pushforwards of quasicoherent sheaves by maps of derived stacks always exist, the functor

\mathscr{Q}\simeq\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}(-,Q^{\otimes}_{1,\bullet}):\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}

satisfies
Assumption

2.24
.

3.1.3.
Aside:
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded quasicoherent sheaves

Fix
A\in\mathscr{CA}\mathrm{lg}^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathbb{K}}
and consider the
\infty
-category
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{A}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded dg-modules over
A
– this can be identified with the dg-category of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
A
-modules, with
A
having the standard even-odd
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading
8
8

8

The underlying module is obtained by taking the direct sum of
A_{j}
for even
j
, in degree
0
, and for odd
j
, in degree
1
.
, localized at the quasi-isomorphisms.
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{A}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is clearly
\mathbb{K}
-linear and stable (the suspension functor swaps the even and odd degrees), and it is also presentably symmetric monoidal under the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded tensor product. We can then consider the functor

F:\mathscr{CA}\mathrm{lg}^{\mathrm{c}}_{\mathbb{K}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}),\quad A\mapsto\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{A}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}.

By
[
Lur18b
, Theorem D.3.5.2, Remark D.3.5.3]
the functor
F
satisfies étale descent. Since
F
also preserves finite products (
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{A\oplus B}\simeq\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{A}\times\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{B}
) we have from
[
CHS22
, Definition B.3.1]
that
F
is an étale sheaf and hence
F
determines a limit preserving functor

\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}:\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}).

Similarly as for
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}
we obtain a representing object

Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}

for
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.

3.1.4.
(Pre)Symplectic derived stacks

Quite a lot of calculus can be developed over special kinds of derived stacks. When we restrict ourselves to the nice subcategory
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{Art}}
of
Artin stacks
(see
[
CHS22
, Definition B.7.4]
) then each
X\in\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{Art}}
has a unique
cotangent complex

\mathbb{L}_{X}\in\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}(X)
(see
[
CHS22
, Definition B.10.2, Theorem B.10.8]
), a dualizable quasicoherent sheaf on
X
which behaves similarly to the cotangent bundle of smooth manifolds; we denote its dual by

\mathbb{T}_{X}:=\mathbb{L}_{X}^{\vee}

and call it the
tangent complex
. When
X=\Spec A
is affine, then
\mathbb{L}_{X}=:\mathbb{L}_{A}\in\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{A}
is the corepresenting object for the functor
\mathrm{Der}_{\mathbb{K}}(A,-)
sending a
\mathbb{K}
-module
M
to the space of derivations
A\to M
(see
[
CHS22
, Section B.2]
). The tangent and cotangent complexes are functorial in
X
: each map
f:Y\to X
induces a map
f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{X}\to\mathbb{L}_{Y}
and a dual map
\mathbb{T}_{Y}\to f^{\ast}\mathbb{T}_{X}
. Moreover, one can define the
p
th exterior power
of the cotangent complex,
\bigwedge^{p}\mathbb{L}_{X}\in\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}(X)
, and the associated
space of
p
-forms on
X
,
\mathcal{A}^{p}(X)\in\mathscr{S}
, which satisfy the equivalence

\mathcal{A}^{p}(X)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\textstyle\bigwedge^{p}\mathbb{L}_{X})\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}(X)}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{p}\mathbb{T}_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})

(see
[
CHS22
, Proposition B.12.2]
). A
p
-form
\omega
together with the data of a closed null-homotopy
d\omega\simeq 0
is a
closed
p
-form
, and these also form a space
\mathcal{A}^{p,\mathrm{cl}}(X)
(see
[
CHS22
, Definition B.12.1]
). The differential
d
is the external de Rham differential (see
[
PTVV13
, Section 1.2]
, appearing as
\epsilon
), not the internal differential on the complex of forms. Both
\mathcal{A}^{p}(X)
and
\mathcal{A}^{p,\mathrm{cl}}(X)
are functorial in
X
and preserve limits so they are represented by some derived stacks
A^{p}
and
A^{p,\mathrm{cl}}
, respectively (see
[
CHS22
, Proposition 12.3]
).

Recall that a symplectic manifold is a smooth manifold
M
together with a closed non-degenerate
2
-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(X)
. A
presymplectic derived stack
is a derived Artin stack
X
together with a closed
2
-form
\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{2,\mathrm{cl}}(X)
on it (see
[
CHS22
, Definition 2.1.3]
). Equivalently, it is a morphism
X\to A^{2,\mathrm{cl}}
whose source is a derived Artin stack, so we can define the
\infty
-category of presymplectic stacks as

\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}:=\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{Art}}\times_{\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}}\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}_{/A^{2,\mathrm{cl}}}

(see
[
CHS22
, Definition 2.3.2]
). This category has a symmetric monoidal structure determined by

(3.1.3)

(X,\omega_{X})\odot(Y,\omega_{Y})\simeq(X\times Y,\pi_{X}^{\ast}\omega_{X}+\pi_{Y}^{\ast}\omega_{Y})

where
\pi_{X}
and
\pi_{Y}
are the projections of
X\times Y
to
X
and
Y
, respectively. The point
\ast=\Spec\mathbb{K}\in\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
has a trivial presymplectic form
\omega_{\ast}=0
(
[
CHS22
, Example 2.1.4]
) and acts as the unit for
\odot
. The forgetful functor
U:\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}^{\odot}\to\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\times}
is symmetric monoidal, as can be easily seen from
Equation

3.1.3
.

Note that

\mathcal{A}^{2}(X)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}(X)}(\mathcal{O}_{X},\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{L}_{X})\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}(X)}(\mathbb{T}_{X},\mathbb{L}_{X})

by the dualizability of
\mathbb{L}_{X}
. If
\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{2}(X)
is a closed
2
-form such that the corresponding map
\mathbb{T}_{X}\to\mathbb{L}_{X}
is an equivalence then the presymplectic stack
(X,\omega)
is called
symplectic
(see
[
CHS22
, Definition 2.1.2]
); this mirrors the non-degeneracy condition for symplectic forms on smooth manifolds.

3.1.5.
Lagrangian correspondences

A span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L,\omega_{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,\omega_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y,\omega_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}
is called an
isotropic correspondence
(see
[
CHS22
, Definition 2.4.3]
). This is really the data of a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{2,\mathrm{cl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{Y}}

in
\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
, with
X,Y,L\in\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}^{\mathrm{Art}}
, which includes the data of an equivalence
f^{\ast}\omega_{X}-g^{\ast}\omega_{Y}\simeq 0
in
\mathcal{A}^{2,\mathrm{cl}}(L)
. If
X\simeq\ast
is the point then
\omega_{X}\simeq 0
and so
0\simeq g^{\ast}\omega_{Y}
, in which case we call
g
an
isotropic morphism
.

Note that an isotropic correspondence as above gives rise to a commutative hexagon

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}_{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\mathbb{T}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}\mathbb{T}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{L}}

in
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}(L)
. We say that the isotropic correspondence is
Lagrangian
if this is a limit diagram (see
[
CHS22
, Definition 2.4.7]
). The definition of a Lagrangian span extends to that of a
Lagrangian
k
-fold span
(see
[
CHS22
, Definition 2.6.11, Definition 2.9.3]
): informally, these are functors
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{k}\to\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}
such that each generating span is a Lagrangian correspondence and each composite, obtained by taking pullbacks, is Lagrangian as well. It was shown that the collection of such spans forms a subcategory
\mathscr{L}\mathrm{ag}
of the
\infty
-category of spans in
\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}
(this is
\mathrm{Lag}^{\ast,0}_{1}
in
[
CHS22
, Section 2.9]
). Thus there is a full subcategory
\widehat{\mathrm{Lag}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp})^{1,0,\mathrm{cart}}
which straightens to the
\infty
-category
\mathscr{L}\mathrm{ag}
.

3.2.
Commutative Rozansky-Witten theories

3.2.1.
Push-pull spans in presymplectic stacks

Set
\mathscr{C}=\mathrm{d}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
and
\mathscr{D}=\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}
be the two
\infty
-categories defined above with their symmetric monoidal forgetful functor
U:\mathscr{D}^{\odot}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}
, and let
Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes}
be the symmetric monoidal category object of
\mathscr{C}
classifying the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded quasicoherent sheaves. From
Definition

2.36
we have a symmetric monoidal
(\infty,3)
-category
\mathscr{PP}(U;Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes})
of relative push-pull spans with the following informal description:

Objects:
The objects of
\mathscr{PP}(U;Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes})
are presymplectic derived stacks
(X,\omega)
. The monoidal product
(X,\omega_{X})\odot(Y,\omega_{Y})
is defined in
Equation

3.1.3
.

1
-morphisms:
The
1
-morphisms are isotropic correspondences
(X,\omega_{X})\leftarrow L\rightarrow(Y,\omega_{Y})
, i.e. spans of presymplectic derived stacks. If
(-)^{\diamondsuit}:\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}\to\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}
denotes the dualization functor taking
(X,\omega)
to
(X,-\omega)
then there is an equivalence

(3.2.1)

\mathscr{PP}(X,Y)\simeq\mathscr{PP}(\ast,X^{\diamondsuit}\odot Y)

for any
X,Y\in\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}
. This follows from the equivalence of Lagrangian structures explained in
[
CHS22
, Section 2.4]
. The composition of two correspondences is given by taking pullbacks, and the identity
1
-morphism
X\to X
is the diagonal correspondence
X\leftarrow X\to X
.

2
-morphisms:
The
2
-morphisms between correspondences
(X,\omega_{X})\leftarrow L\rightarrow(Y,\omega_{Y})
and
(X,\omega_{X})\leftarrow M\rightarrow(Y,\omega_{Y})
are given by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded quasicoherent sheaves over the derived intersection
L\cap M:=L\times_{X\times Y}M
of
L
and
M
. The horizontal composition is simply given by pulling back and tensoring: given composable spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}

and sheaves
\mathcal{M}\in\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(L\cap M)
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(L^{\prime}\cap M^{\prime})
, their composite is

\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{M}\otimes(\pi^{\prime})^{\ast}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}((L\times_{Y}L^{\prime})\cap(M\times_{Y}M^{\prime}))

where
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are the canonical projections as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L\times_{Y}L^{\prime})\cap(M\times_{Y}M^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L\cap M)\times_{Y}(L^{\prime}\cap M^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\cap M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}\cap M^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}

The identity for this composition is the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X\times X}\in\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X\cap X)=\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(X\times X)
: in fact, given a sheaf
\mathcal{M}\in\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(L\cap M)
where
L
and
M
are Lagrangian correspondences from
X
to
Y
we have that the projections
\pi:(L\times_{Y}Y)\times(M\times_{Y}Y)\to L\cap M
and
\pi^{\prime}:(L\times_{Y}Y)\times(M\times_{Y}Y)\to Y\cap Y=Y\times Y
are none other than the identity map in the first case and the natural projection in the second case, so

\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{M}\otimes(\pi^{\prime})^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{Y\times Y}\simeq\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{L\cap M}\simeq\mathcal{M}.

The vertical composition is given by the push-pull formula: given three spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}

and sheaves
\mathcal{M}\in\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(L\cap M)
and
\mathcal{N}\in\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(M\cap N)
, their composite is

i_{M,\ast}(i_{N}^{\ast}\mathcal{M}\otimes i_{L}^{\ast}\mathcal{N})\in\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(L\cap N)

where

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\cap M\cap N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\cap N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\cap N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\cap M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{N}}

are the three canonical inclusions of derived intersections. The identity for this composition is given by
\nabla_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{L}
, where
\nabla:L\to L\cap L=L\times_{X\times Y}L
is the diagonal map. This follows from a quick computation: since the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\cap M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\cap M\cap M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\cap M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}

commutes and
\nabla
satisfies the base change and the projection formula (see
[
CHS22
, Definitions B.8.1, B.8.2, and Theorem B.8.12]
, if
\mathcal{M}\in\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(L\cap M)
is another sheaf then

\displaystyle\nabla_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{M}\circ\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle:=i_{M,\ast}(i_{M}^{\ast}\mathcal{M}\otimes i_{L}^{\ast}\nabla_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{M})

\displaystyle\simeq i_{M,\ast}(i_{M}^{\ast}\mathcal{M}\otimes\nabla_{\ast}j^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{M})

(base change)

\displaystyle\simeq i_{M,\ast}\nabla_{\ast}(\nabla^{\ast}i_{M}^{\ast}\mathcal{M}\otimes j^{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{M})

(projection)

\displaystyle\simeq(i_{M}\circ\nabla)_{\ast}((i_{M}\circ\nabla)^{\ast}\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{L\cap M})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle(i_{M}\circ\nabla\simeq\mathrm{id}_{L\cap M})

and similarly for the other identity.

3
-morphisms:
The
3
-morphisms are given by maps of sheaves over a span: a
3
-morphism between two sheaves
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
over
L\cap M
is given by a span
L\cap M\xlongleftarrow{f}N\xlongrightarrow{g}L\cap M
and a morphism
\alpha:f^{\ast}\mathcal{M}\to g^{\ast}\mathcal{N}
in
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(N)
. If we reduce to the spans of the form

L\cap M\xlongleftarrow{\mathrm{id}}L\cap M\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}L\cap M

then we obtain the space of maps between
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
in
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(L\cap M)
. The horizontal composition is given by pulling back and tensoring in the only way which is compatible with the composition of
1
-morphisms; the vertical composition is given by the push-pull formula for the vertical composition of
2
-morphisms, but applied to the morphisms; the transversal composition is simply the composition of maps of sheaves after suitable pullbacks. The identities for these compositions are the obvious ones induced by the formulas above.

3.2.2.
Further restrictions

With a little bit of care we can restrict to a smaller
(\infty,3)
-category which better approximates the idea in
[
KR10
]
. Consider the projections

\displaystyle\sigma:\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes})_{t,k,n,l}

\displaystyle\to\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}^{\times tk(n+1)(l+1)}

\displaystyle\lambda:\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes})_{t,k,n,l}

\displaystyle\to(\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp})^{1}_{k})^{\times t(n+1)(l+1)}

\displaystyle\rho:\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes})_{t,k,n,l}

\displaystyle\to(\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp})^{1}_{n})^{\times t(k+1)(l+1)}

obtained by composing various Segal maps:
\sigma
sends a generalized span to the collection of its generating objects in
\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}
,
\lambda
sends it to the collection of its horizontal slices (spans in
\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}
underlying the composition of
1
-morphisms), and
\rho
send it to the collection of its transversal slices (spans in
\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp}
underlying the composition of
3
-morphisms). Since
\widehat{\mathrm{Lag}}_{k}
is contained in
\widehat{\mathrm{GS}}(\mathscr{P}\mathrm{re}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ymp})^{1}_{k}
we can make the following definitions:

Definition 3.2
.

A generalized span
(F,\eta)\in\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes})_{t,k,n,l}

(1)

has symplectic objects
if
\sigma(F)
is a tuple of symplectic derived stacks,

(2)

has Lagrangian
1
-morphisms
if
\lambda(F)
is in
(\widehat{\mathrm{Lag}}_{k})^{\times t(n+1)(l+1)}
,

(3)

is horizontally Lagrangian
if it has both symplectic objects and Lagrangian
1
-morphisms, and

(4)

has standard maps of sheaves
if every component of
\rho(F)
is a constant iterated span, i.e. all the projection morphisms are equivalences.

Denote by
\widehat{\mathscr{CRW}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes})
the full subcategory spanned by the generalized spans which are horizontally Lagrangian and have standard maps of sheaves.

Being horizontally Lagrangian implies that, in our desired
(\infty,3)
-category, the objects are all symplectic stacks and the
1
-morphisms are all Lagrangian correspondences. Having standard maps of sheaves implies that if
L
and
M
are (now Lagrangian) spans between (now symplectic) stacks
X
and
Y
, then the
\infty
-category of
2
-morphisms between
L
and
M
is precisely
\mathscr{QC}\mathrm{oh}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(L\cap M)
. We will conclude this section by proving that enforcing these properties still returns an
(\infty,3)
-category.

Proposition 3.3
.

All four properties in
Definition

3.2
are stable under pulling back by maps in
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{3,{\mathrm{op}}}
.

Proof.

It’s easy to see that the fourth property is stable since equivalences are preserved by pullbacks. Pulling back by maps in the second and third
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
factors simply changes the number of objects of
k
-folds spans under consideration, and this does not affect the properties. Pulling back by maps in
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
amounts to taking products of spans or inserting trivial spans; taking products preserves both the symplectic property and the Lagrangian property (see
[
CHS22
, Section 2.10]
) and so we’re done. Finally, the maps in the first
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
factor induce the structure maps of
\mathrm{Lag}
; the latter is obviously closed under these structure maps since it forms an
\infty
-category.
∎

Corollary 3.4
.

The projection
\widehat{\mathscr{CRW}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes})\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{3,{\mathrm{op}}}
is a cocartesian fibration.

Proof.

Follows immediately from the previous proposition since the inclusion of
\widehat{\mathscr{CRW}}
into
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes})
is full – compare, for example, with the proof of
Corollary

2.12
.
∎

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\mathscr{CRW}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{3,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
denote a straightening of the projection
\widehat{\mathscr{CRW}}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{3,{\mathrm{op}}}
, which exists by
Corollary

3.4
.

Proposition 3.6
.

The functor
\mathscr{CRW}
satisfies the Segal condition in all four variables.

Proof.

It is easy to see that the Segal maps for
\widehat{\mathscr{CRW}}
are pulled back from the Segal maps for
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes})
along the inclusion
\widehat{\mathscr{CRW}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes})
: in fact, the projections
\sigma,\lambda,\rho
in
Definition

3.2
are the composition of the Segal maps for the first, third, and fourth variable variables followed by further projections, which are themselves stable under pullbacks. Since the Segal maps for
\widehat{\mathrm{PP}}(U;Q^{\mathbb{Z}_{2},\otimes})
are equivalences by the results of
Section

2.4
we obtain that the Segal maps for
\widehat{\mathscr{CRW}}
are also equivalences, proving the claim.
∎

Corollary 3.7
.

The functor
\mathscr{CRW}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{3,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
determines a symmetric monoidal
(\infty,3)
-category. This is denoted
\mathscr{CRW}
, and we call it the
(\infty,3)
-category of commutative Rozansky-Witten theories
.

Proof.

Combine
Proposition

3.6
with the construction of a complete
3
-fold Segal space from a
3
-uple Segal space.
∎

Appendix A
Higher categorical background

Here we collect a list of definitions and results about higher categries and higher algebra that we need for
Sections

2
and
3
. Some results are well known, but we wrote down a proof of those for which we could not track down a precise reference.

A.1.
Functors versus fibrations

Throughout this work we make extensive use of Lurie’s
straightening/unstraightening equivalence
, which asserts that for any
\infty
-category
\mathscr{D}
there is an adjoint equivalence

(A.1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}}_{/\mathscr{D}}^{\mathrm{cocart}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Un}}

\dashv

between the category of functors
\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
and the category of cocartesian fibrations with target
\mathscr{D}
and maps preserving cocartesian morphisms. See
[
Lur09a
, Section 2]
for information about cocartesian fibrations and
[
Lur09a
, Section 3.2]
or
[
HHR21
]
for a detailed proof of straightening/unstraightening.

For our purposes it will be sufficient to have just a few properties of this equivalence. Recall:

Definition A.1
.

Let
P:\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{D}
be a functor and
f:e_{1}\to e_{2}
a morphism in
\mathscr{E}
. Then
f
is
P
-cocartesian
(or just
cocartesian
if
P
can be inferred from the context) if for every
e_{3}\in\mathscr{E}
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}(e_{2},e_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}(e_{1},e_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}(P(e_{2}),P(e_{3}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}(P(e_{1}),P(e_{3}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(f)^{\ast}}

is a pullback square, or equivalently the diagram of undercategories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}_{e_{2}/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}_{e_{1}/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}_{P(e_{2})/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}_{P(e_{1})/}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)^{\ast}}

is a pullback square. We also say that
P
is a
cocartesian fibration
if for every
g:d_{1}\to d_{2}
in
\mathscr{D}
and every
e_{1}\in\mathscr{E}
with
P(e_{1})\simeq d_{1}
there is a cocartesian morphism
f
in
\mathscr{E}
such that
P(f)\simeq g
. If
P^{\mathrm{op}}
is a cocartesian fibration then
P
is a
cartesian fibration
.

If
g:d_{1}\to d_{2}
is a morphism in
\mathscr{D}
,
e_{1}
is a lift of
d_{1}
, and
f
is a cocartesian lift of
g
starting at
e_{1}
, we sometimes denote the target of
f
by
g_{!}(e_{1})
. This is meant to signify that if the cocartesian fibration
P
comes from a functor
F:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
via
Equation

A.1.1
then
g_{!}(e_{1})\simeq F(g)(e_{1})
in the
\infty
-category
F(d_{2})
. In particular, precomposing by
f
gives an equivalence

\mathscr{E}_{/\mathrm{id}_{d_{2}}}(g_{!}(e_{1}),e^{\prime})\simeq\mathscr{E}_{/g}(e_{1},e^{\prime})

for any lift
e^{\prime}
of
d_{2}
and this can be used to show that any two cocartesian lifts of
g
have equivalent targets (i.e.
g_{!}(e_{1})
is essentially unique).

The equivalence in
Equation

A.1.1
is functorial in
\mathscr{D}
: if
G:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
and
F:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
are functors then

\mathrm{Un}(F\circ G)\simeq\mathscr{C}\times_{\mathscr{D}}\mathrm{Un}(F)

as cocartesian fibrations over
\mathscr{C}
. In particular when
G=\{d\}:\Delta^{0}\to\mathscr{D}
picks out an object
d\in\mathscr{D}
