then we have an equivalence of
\infty
-categories between the fiber of
\mathrm{Un}(F)
at
d
and the value
F(d)
:

F(d)\simeq\mathrm{Un}(F\circ\{d\})\simeq\Delta^{0}\times_{\mathscr{D}}\mathrm{Un}(F)\simeq\mathrm{Un}(F)_{d}.

Passing to
\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}
gives an an equivalence for cartesian fibrations instead, and replacing
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
with
\mathscr{S}
gives another version for left and right fibrations. This equivalence happens inside an
\infty
-category of large
\infty
-categories within which
\mathscr{D}
,
{\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}}_{/\mathscr{D}}^{\mathrm{cocart}}
,
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
, and
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1})
live. We will not dwell too much on size issues, but the interested reader can assume that we’re fixing a hierarchy of Grothendieck universes associated to cardinals
\kappa_{1}<\kappa_{2}<\dotsb
and using “small” to refer to
\kappa_{1}
-small
\infty
-categories, “large” to refer to
\kappa_{2}
-small
\infty
-categories, and “very large” for the subsequent ones.

Given a functor
F:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
we will denote by
\widehat{F}\to\mathscr{D}
or
\widehat{F}\to\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}
the associated cocartesian or cartesian fibration; the variance is usually implicit from the context, but when we need to disambiguate between the two we will write
\mathrm{Un}(F)\to\mathscr{D}
for the cocartesian unstraightening and
\mathrm{Un}^{\mathrm{op}}(F)\to\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}
for the cartesian unstraightening. By
[
GHN17
, Theorem 7.4, Theorem 7.6]
we have equivalences

\mathrm{Un}(F)\simeq\int^{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}F(-)\times\mathscr{C}_{(-)/},\quad\mathrm{Un}^{\mathrm{op}}(F)\simeq\int^{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}F(-)\times(\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}})_{/(-)}

(see
Section

A.3
for the notation). At the level of ordinary categories (i.e. when
\mathscr{D}=\mathbf{D}
is ordinary and
F
lands in
\mathbf{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
) the above formulas turn into explicit constructions for
\widehat{F}
, the
Grothendieck (op)fibration associated to
F
or
category of elements of
F
, in terms of objects of
\mathbf{D}
and elements of the categories
F(d)
for
d\in\mathbf{D}
.

If
\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{D}
is a cocartesian fibration and
f:x\to y
is a morphism in
\mathscr{D}
then we denote the induced functor on fibers by
f_{\ast}:\mathscr{E}_{x}\to\mathscr{E}_{y}
. If
\mathscr{D}=\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
f:x\to y
is a morphism in
\mathscr{C}
then we denote the induced functor by
f^{\ast}:\mathscr{E}_{y}\to\mathscr{E}_{x}
instead.

A.2.
A model for higher categories

Here we briefly discuss the model for
(\infty,n)
-categories that will be used throughout the paper. The following material is presented in great detail (including the relevant sources) in Sections 3, 4, and 7 of
[
Hau18
]
; we direct the interested reader to it for a more complete exposition. For the remainder of this section,
\mathscr{D}
is an
\infty
-category with finite limits.

Definition A.2
.

A simplicial object
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{D}
is called a
category object
in
\mathscr{D}
if, for any
n\geq 0
, the maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(j,j+1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{j}}

in
\mathbf{\Delta}
induce an equivalence

(\rho_{0}\times_{\sigma_{1}}\dotsb\times_{\sigma_{n-1}}\rho_{n-1})_{\ast}:X_{n}\xlongrightarrow{\simeq}X_{1}\times_{X_{0}}\dotsb\times_{X_{0}}X_{1}

in
\mathscr{D}
, where the right-hand side contains
n
copies of
X_{1}
. The above equation is called a
Segal condition
. The full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{D})
spanned by the category objects is denoted by
\mathrm{Cat}(\mathscr{D})
. A
Segal space
is a category object in
\mathscr{S}
.

The data of a Segal space
X
includes a space
X_{0}
of “objects”
x,y,z,\dotsc
and a space
X_{1}
of “morphisms”
f,g,h,\dotsc
, each of which has a source
s:X_{1}\to X_{0}
and target
t:X_{1}\to X_{0}
determined by the two maps
\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1}:[0]\to[1]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
. The inclusion
[1]\to[2]
defined by
c(0)=0
and
c(1)=2
produces a map
c:X_{2}\to X_{1}
and thus the Segal condition guarantees that there is a “composition” operation
X_{1}\times_{X_{0}}X_{1}\xleftarrow{\simeq}X_{2}\xlongrightarrow{c}X_{1}
, defined up to equivalence, sending a pair
(f,g)
of composable edges to a candidate for the composite
g\circ f\in X_{1}
. The space of these candidates is contractible and so, up to equivalence,
g\circ f
is unique. The assumption that the Segal condition holds for all other values of
n
guarantees that this composition is unital and associative up to equivalence.

Given a category object
X\in\mathrm{Cat}(\mathscr{D})
and an object
d\in\mathscr{D}
we obtain a Segal space
X(d)\in\mathrm{Cat}(\mathscr{S})
by taking the composite

\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\xlongrightarrow{X}\mathscr{D}\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}}(d,-)}\mathscr{S}.

It will be helpful to think of an arbitrary category object in this way, as a representing object for Segal spaces parametrized (contravariantly) by
\mathscr{D}
.

Note that, for any
\infty
-category
\mathscr{A}
, limits in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{D})
are computed componentwise in
\mathscr{D}
so
\mathrm{Cat}(\mathscr{D})
is again an
\infty
-category with finite limits.

Definition A.3
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Cat}^{n}(\mathscr{D})
of
n
-uple category objects
in
\mathscr{D}
is defined inductively as follows:

•

\mathrm{Cat}^{0}(\mathscr{D}):=\mathscr{D}
;

•

\mathrm{Cat}^{n}(\mathscr{D}):=\mathrm{Cat}(\mathrm{Cat}^{n-1}(\mathscr{D}))
for
n\geq 1
.

An
n
-uple Segal space
is an
n
-uple category object in
\mathscr{S}
. If
X
is an
n
-uple Segal space and
a
is an
n
-tuple of
0
s and
1
s containing exactly
k
many
1
s, then a vertex of the space
X_{a}
is called a
k
-morphism
of
X
; a
0
-morphism is also called an
object
of
X
.

By unwinding the definition we see that an
n
-uple category object in
\mathscr{D}
is equivalently a functor
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{D}
which satisfies the Segal condition in each variable independently. The data of
X
can be thought of as a collection of
n
-dimensional cubes: the legs of each cube are finite sequences of composable
1
-morphisms between objects, and the higher dimensional faces are represented by the
k
-morphisms glued along their common
(k-1)
-morphisms. Here is a picture of a
2
-cube in
X_{m,n}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{m,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{m,1}}

\dotsb

\dotsb

\dotsb

\dotsb

\dotsb

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{2,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dotsb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{m,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m,n}}

The commutative squares here contain the data represented by
X_{1,1}
.

Note that an
n
-uple Segal space has exactly
\binom{n}{k}
many possibly different kinds of
k
-morphisms. To pass from the “cubical” data of an
n
-uple category to the “globular” data of an
n
-category we must enforce the condition that there be only one kind of
k
-morphism for every
k
, so that we can talk about
(k+1)
-morphisms as going from one
k
-morphism to another. This degeneracy condition on the
k
-morphisms is encoded by letting certain representing objects be trivial or constant. In general we say that an
n
-uple category object
X
is
essentially constant
if it the canonical natural transformation
c_{X_{0,\dotsc,0}}\to X
, where
c_{X_{0,\dotsc,0}}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{D}
is the constant functor with value
X_{0,\dotsc,0}
, is an equivalence.

Definition A.4
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{D})
of
n
-fold Segal objects
in
\mathscr{D}
is defined inductively as follows:

•

\mathrm{Seg}^{0}(\mathscr{D}):=\mathscr{D}
;

•

\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{D})\subseteq\mathrm{Cat}(\mathrm{Seg}^{n-1}(\mathscr{D}))
is the full subcategory spanned by the functors
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Seg}^{n-1}(\mathscr{D})
such that
X_{0}
is essentially constant as an
(n-1)
-uple category object in
\mathscr{D}
.

An
n
-fold Segal space
is an
n
-fold Segal object in
\mathscr{S}
.

Up to equivalence, the data of an
n
-fold Segal object reduces to that of the objects
X_{1,\dotsc,1,0,\dotsc,0}
(
k
many
1
s) representing the
k
-morphisms and the induced source, target, composition, identity, and higher associativity maps.

We might be tempted at this point to use
n
-fold Segal spaces as our models of
(\infty,n)
-categories. However, as it turns out, the homotopy theory of
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{S})
does not reflect the desired homotopy theory of
(\infty,n)
-categories in that fully faithful and essentially surjective maps of
n
-fold Segal spaces, adapted to that setting, are not necessarily equivalences. Localizing with respect to those maps gives the correct model of
(\infty,n)
-categories, which also happens to be equivalent to the subcategory of
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{S})
spanned by those
n
-fold Segal spaces
X
which are
complete
, meaning that all the equivalences internal to the
n
-fold category
X
arise through degeneracies from the space of objects
X_{0,\dotsc,0}
; the case
n=1
was proved by Joyal and Tierney in
[
JT07
]
, and Barwick generalized the result to
n>1
in his thesis.

Definition A.5
.

An
(\infty,n)
-category
is a complete
n
-fold Segal space. We denote by
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{n}\subseteq\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{S})
the full subcategory spanned by the
(\infty,n)
-categories.

The following proposition allows us to bypass checking for completeness when building
(\infty,n)
-categories:

Proposition A.6
.

The inclusion
\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{S})\subseteq\mathrm{Cat}^{n}(\mathscr{S})
admits a right adjoint
R^{n}:\mathrm{Cat}^{n}(\mathscr{S})\to\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{S})
– the
underlying
n
-fold Segal space
– and the inclusion
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{n}\subseteq\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{S})
admits a left adjoint
L^{n}:\mathrm{Seg}^{n}(\mathscr{S})\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{n}
– the
free complete
n
-fold Segal space
– which preserves products. Therefore we obtain a product-preserving functor

\mathcal{U}^{n}=L^{n}\circ R^{n}:\mathrm{Cat}^{n}(\mathscr{S})\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{n}.

Proof.

The first three assertions are from Proposition 4.12, Definition 7.4, and Proposition 7.10 in
[
Hau18
]
. Since
R^{n}
preserves all limits and
L^{n}
preserves products we see that
\mathcal{U}^{n}
preserves products.
∎

A.3.
Ends in
\infty
-categories

In ordinary category theory, the
end
of a bifunctor
F:\mathbf{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C}
is the equalizer

\int_{\mathbf{A}}F=\int_{a\in\mathbf{A}}F(a,a):=\mathrm{eq}\left(\prod_{a\in\mathbf{A}}F(a,a)\rightrightarrows\prod_{a\to a^{\prime}}F(a,a^{\prime})\right),

which is equivalent to a limit over the twisted arrow category of
\mathbf{A}
– see
[
Lor21
, Section 1]
. The latter definition readily translates to the
\infty
-categorical context:

Definition A.7
.

Given a bifunctor
F:\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}
of
\infty
-categories we define its
end
to be the limit

\int_{\mathscr{A}}F=\int_{a\in\mathscr{A}}F(a,a):=\lim(\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{A}\xlongrightarrow{F}\mathscr{C}).

Here
\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{A})\to\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{A}
is a left fibration
9
9

9

Warning: our
\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{A})
is defined as
\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{A})^{\mathrm{op}}
in
[
GHN17
]
. Since we are borrowing results from that paper, we encourage the readers who are comparing the two works to pay attention to the change in notation.
corresponding to the bifunctor
\mathscr{A}(-,-):\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{S}
.

The dual notion is that of a
coend
:

\int^{\mathscr{A}}F:=\colim(\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{A}\xlongrightarrow{F}\mathscr{C}).

A.3.1.
Useful results about ends

Mirroring the standard result for ordinary categories, Proposition 5.1 in
[
GHN17
]
shows that if
F,G:\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}
are two functors of
\infty
-categories then

(A.3.1)

\mathrm{Nat}(F,G)\simeq\int_{\mathscr{A}}\mathscr{C}(F,G)=\int_{a\in\mathscr{A}}\mathscr{C}(F(a),G(a)).

This can be used to prove an
\infty
-categorical analogue of the classical formula for Kan extensions using ends (see
[
Lor21
]
).

Proposition A.8
.

Let
F:\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{B}
and
G:\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}
be functors of
\infty
-categories. If a right Kan extension of
G
along
F
at
b\in B
exists, then

(\mathrm{RKan}_{F}G)(b)\simeq\int_{a\in\mathscr{A}}\mathscr{B}(b,F(a))\pitchfork G(a).

Proof.

We first recall (see for example
[
Lur18a
, Section 7.3]
) that right Kan extensions are given by the formula

(A.3.2)

(\mathrm{RKan}_{F}G)(b)\simeq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathscr{A}\\
(b\to F(a))\in\mathscr{B}\end{subarray}}G(a),

where the limit is taken over the
\infty
-category

\mathscr{B}_{b/}\times_{\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{A}.

Let
c\in\mathscr{C}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(c,(\mathrm{RKan}_{F}G)(b))

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathscr{A}\\
(b\to F(a))\in\mathscr{B}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{C}(c,G(a))

(a)

\displaystyle=\lim\left(\mathscr{B}_{b/}\times_{\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{A}\xlongrightarrow{\pi}\mathscr{A}\xlongrightarrow{\mathscr{C}(c,G(-))}\mathscr{S}\right).

We will show that the right-hand side is equivalent to

\mathrm{Nat}(\mathscr{B}(b,F(-)),\mathscr{C}(c,G(-))),

the space of natural transformations of functors
\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{S}
between
\mathscr{B}(b,F(-))
and
\mathscr{C}(c,G(-))
. Notice first that
\pi
is a left fibration classifying
\mathscr{B}(b,F(-))
. If
\omega:\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{A}
is a left fibration that classifies
\mathscr{C}(c,G(-))
, then

\pi\times_{\mathscr{A}}\omega:(\mathscr{B}_{b/}\times_{\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{A})\times_{\mathscr{A}}\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{A}

is a left fibration that classifies the composite
\mathscr{C}(c,G(\pi(-))):\mathscr{B}_{b/}\times_{\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{S}
since the unstraightening functor takes pre-composition to pullbacks. Hence

\displaystyle\mathrm{Nat}(\mathscr{B}(b,F(-)),\mathscr{C}(c,G(-)))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{cocart}}_{/\mathscr{A}}(\mathscr{B}_{b/}\times_{\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{A},\mathscr{E})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{cocart}}_{/(\mathscr{B}_{b/}\times_{\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{A})}(\mathscr{B}_{b/}\times_{\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{A},(\mathscr{B}_{b/}\times_{\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{A})\times_{\mathscr{A}}\mathscr{E})

\displaystyle\simeq\lim\left(\mathscr{B}_{b/}\times_{\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{A}\xlongrightarrow{\mathscr{C}(c,G(\pi(-)))}\mathscr{S}\right),

where
\mathrm{Map}^{\mathrm{cocart}}_{/\mathscr{D}}
denotes the space of maps in
{\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}}^{\mathrm{cocart}}_{/\mathscr{D}}
and the last equivalence follows from
[
Lur09a
, Corollary 3.3.3.4]
, which states that the space of sections of a left fibration is equivalent to the limit of the functor it classifies.

Now we can apply the end formula
Equation

A.3.1
for
\mathrm{Nat}(-,-)
:

\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathscr{A}\\
(b\to F(a))\in\mathscr{B}\end{subarray}}\mathscr{C}(c,G(a))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Nat}(\mathscr{B}(b,F(-)),\mathscr{C}(c,G(-)))

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{a\in\mathscr{A}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{S}}(\mathscr{B}(b,F(a)),\mathscr{C}(c,G(a)))

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{a\in\mathscr{A}}\mathscr{C}(c,\mathscr{B}(b,F(a))\pitchfork G(a))

(b)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathscr{C}\left(c,\int_{a\in\mathscr{A}}\mathscr{B}(b,F(a))\pitchfork G(a)\right).

The claim follows if we combine the two equivalences (a) and (b) and then apply the Yoneda lemma.
∎

Corollary A.9
.

Assume
F:\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{S}
and
G:\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}
are functors. Then

\int_{\mathscr{A}}F\pitchfork G\simeq(\mathrm{RKan}_{y}G)(F)\simeq\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathscr{A}\\
x\in F(a)\end{subarray}}G(a)\quad\left(\simeq\lim(\mathscr{A}_{F/}\to\mathscr{A}\xlongrightarrow{G}\mathscr{C})\right)

where
y:\mathscr{A}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{S})^{\mathrm{op}}
is the co-Yoneda embedding given on objects by
a\mapsto\mathscr{A}(a,-)
, and
\mathscr{A}_{F/}
falls into the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{F/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{S})^{\mathrm{op}}_{F/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{S})^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}

of
\infty
-categories.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{B}=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{C})^{\mathrm{op}}
. Then

\int_{\mathscr{A}}F\pitchfork G=\int_{a\in\mathscr{A}}F(a)\pitchfork G(a)\simeq\int_{a\in\mathscr{A}}\mathscr{B}(F,y(a))\pitchfork G(a)\simeq(\mathrm{RKan}_{y}G)(F)

where the second equivalence comes from the co-Yoneda lemma:
F(a)\simeq\mathscr{B}(F,y(a))
naturally in
a\in\mathscr{A}
. The limit formula for right Kan extensions (
[
Lur18a
, Definition 7.3.1.2]
) provides the last equivalence in the claim, and the shorthand for the limit comes from the Yoneda lemma since morphisms
x:F\to y(a)
in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{S})^{\mathrm{op}}
exactly correspond to vertices of
x\in F(a)
.
∎

For ends of this form, given any
a\in\mathscr{A}
and
c\in\mathscr{C}
we have a component map

(A.3.3)

\mathscr{C}\left(c,\int_{\mathscr{A}}F\pitchfork G\right)\simeq\int_{\mathscr{A}}\mathscr{S}(F,\mathscr{C}(c,G))\simeq\mathrm{Nat}(F,\mathscr{C}(c,G))\\
\to\mathscr{S}(F(a),\mathscr{C}(c,G(a)))\to\mathscr{C}(c,F(a)\pitchfork G(a))

which allows us to pick specific morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
represented by the end.

We conclude with a simplification for certain kinds of ends.

Proposition A.10
.

Let
\mathscr{D}=\mathscr{D}_{0}^{\lhd}
be the cone on an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{D}_{0}
and denote the initial object of
\mathscr{D}
by
i
. Moreover, let
F:\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
be a functor and let
F_{0}
denote the restriction of
F
to
\mathscr{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{D}_{0}
. Then we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{D}}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}F_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(i,i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\lim_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}F(i,-)}

where the top map is the canonical restriction map induced by the inclusion
\mathscr{D}_{0}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
, the vertical maps are two component maps coming from the limit, and the bottom map is induced by post-composition in
\mathscr{D}
.

Proof.

Consider the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}_{0}\times\Delta^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\times\{0\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{i\}}

which identifies
\mathscr{D}
with a cone on
\mathscr{D}_{0}
. Since
\mathrm{Tw}:\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
preserves colimits (see
[
Lur18a
, Remark 8.1.1.4]
) we have an induced pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D}_{0}\times\Delta^{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tw}(\Delta^{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

If we set
\overline{F}:=\pi\circ F:\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D})\to\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
then
[
Lur18a
, Proposition 7.6.3.26]
gives a
pullback
square

(A.3.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim\overline{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim\overline{F}|_{\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D}_{0}\times\Delta^{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim\overline{F}|_{\mathrm{Tw}(\Delta^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim\overline{F}|_{\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D}_{0})}}

of limits of
\overline{F}
restricted to the various
\infty
-categories in the diagram. We will now identify the four corners of this pullback square and show that it reduces to the diagram in the claim.

•

By definition we have
\lim\overline{F}=\int_{\mathscr{D}}F
.

•

Since
\mathrm{Tw}(\Delta^{0})\simeq\Delta^{0}
and the map
\delta:\mathrm{Tw}(\Delta^{0})\to\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D})
is the inclusion of the object
\mathrm{id}_{i}
, the identity map of the initial object
i
, the limit
\lim\overline{F}|_{\mathrm{Tw}(\Delta^{0})}
is equivalent to
\overline{F}(\mathrm{id}_{i})=F(i,i)
.

•

The map
\delta\circ\beta:\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D}_{0})\to\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D})
(and hence also
\gamma\circ\alpha
, since the two are equivalent) factors through
\beta
and thus
\overline{F}|_{\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D}_{0})}
is the constant functor at
F(i,i)
. Alternatively we can write it as the composite functor

\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D}_{0})\to\mathscr{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{D}_{0}\to\mathscr{D}_{0}\to\mathscr{C}

where the last map
\mathscr{D}_{0}\to\mathscr{C}
is the constant functor
c_{F(i,i)}
and so

\lim\overline{F}|_{\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D}_{0})}\simeq\int_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}c_{F(i,i)}.

The end of a bifunctor
H:\mathscr{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{D}_{0}\to\mathscr{C}
which factors through the projection to
\mathscr{D}_{0}
is simply the limit of the functor out of
\mathscr{D}_{0}
, so

\lim\overline{F}|_{\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D}_{0})}\simeq\int_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}c_{F(i,i)}=\lim_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}c_{F(i,i)}.

•

The map
g:\mathscr{D}_{0}\times\Delta^{1}\to\mathscr{D}
is given on objects by

g(x,n)=\begin{cases}i&n=0,\\
x&n=1,\end{cases}

where
x\in\mathscr{D}_{0}
and
n\in\{0,1\}
stands for one of the two objects of
\Delta^{1}
. This means that

\lim\overline{F}|_{\mathrm{Tw}(\mathscr{D}_{0}\times\Delta^{1})}\simeq\int_{\mathscr{D}_{0}\times\Delta^{1}}F(g(-),g(-))\simeq\int_{\Delta^{1}}\int_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}F(g(-),g(-))

where the last equivalence follows from the Fubini theorem for ends (see, for example,
[
Hau18
, Proposition 2.21]
). It can be easily calculated that
\mathrm{Tw}(\Delta^{1})
is isomorphic to the ordinary cospan

\bullet\to\bullet\leftarrow\bullet

where, in terms of morphisms in
\Delta^{1}
, the left and right points correspond to
\mathrm{id}_{0}
and
\mathrm{id}_{1}
and the middle point corresponds to the unique nontrivial morphism
e:0\to 1
. This readily implies that we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\int_{\Delta^{1}}\int_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}F(g(-),g(-))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}F(-,-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}F(i,i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}F(i,-)}

which simplifies to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\int_{\Delta^{1}}\int_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}F(g(-),g(-))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}F_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\lim_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}c_{F(i,i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\lim_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}F(i,-)}

After substituting each limit and simplifying, the pullback square in
Equation

A.3.4
turns into the desired square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{D}}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\int_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}F_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(i,i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\lim_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}F(i,-)}

concluding the proof.
∎

Corollary A.11
.

Let
\mathscr{D}=\mathscr{D}_{0}^{\lhd}
be the cone on an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{D}_{0}
and denote the initial object of
\mathscr{D}
by
i
. If
F,G:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
are two functors of
\infty
-categories and
F_{0},G_{0}
are their restrictions to
\mathscr{D}_{0}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
then there is a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Nat}(F,G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Nat}(F_{0},G_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(F(i),G(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\lim_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{C}(F(i),G(-))}

Proof.

The statement follows immediately by the previous proposition and the end formula for natural transformations.
∎

Informally, the data of a natural transformation
\eta:F\to G
is equivalent to that of its restriction
\eta_{0}:F_{0}\to G_{0}
together with an extra component map
F(i)\to G(i)
that is compatible with the existing components
F(x)\to G(x)
, for
x\in\mathscr{D}_{0}
. It would be interesting to have a similar formula in the more general case where
\mathscr{D}_{0}
is obtained from
\mathscr{D}
by removing an arbitrary object
d
(in our case we removed the very special initial object
i
).

A.4.
Commutative monoids

Definition A.12
.

A
symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
is a functor
\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
such that the Segal maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle n\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle 1\rangle,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
if
k\neq j
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{j}}

in
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
induce an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}_{n}\xlongrightarrow{\simeq}(\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}_{1})^{\times n}.

This is also called a
Segal condition
. The
underlying category
of
\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}
is
\mathscr{C}:=\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}_{1}
. We say that an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{D}

admits a symmetric monoidal structure
if there exists a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathscr{D}^{\otimes}
with underlying category
\mathscr{D}^{\otimes}_{1}\simeq\mathscr{D}
.

If
\mathscr{C}
is an
\infty
-category with finite limits then it admits a symmetric monoidal structure, called the
cartesian monoidal structure
, and we denote the associated functor by
\mathscr{C}^{\times}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
. The construction of this functor can be found in
[
Lur17
, Section 2.4.1]
.

Definition A.13
.

A
commutative monoid
or
commutative algebra
in a cartesian monoidal category
\mathscr{C}
is a functor
M^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}
satisfying the Segal conditions. We denote by
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})
the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{C})
spanned by the commutative monoids in
\mathscr{C}
.

We have an auxiliary characterization of commutative monoids that will turn out to be useful later. We recall that an
inert morphism
in
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a map
f:\langle m\rangle\to\langle n\rangle
such that
f^{-1}(k)
is a singleton for each
k\neq\ast
.

Proposition A.14
.

Commutative monoids
M^{\otimes}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})
are equivalent to sections
\widetilde{M}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\widehat{\mathscr{C}^{\times}}
of a cocartesian fibration
\widehat{\mathscr{C}^{\times}}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
which straightens to
\mathscr{C}^{\times}
such that
\widetilde{M}
sends inert morphisms to cocartesian morphisms.

Proof.

A more general statement, Proposition 2.4.2.5 in
[
Lur17
]
, is true for operads and this is the case
\mathscr{O}=\mathrm{Comm}\simeq\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
. The condition on inert morphisms is embedded in the definition of maps of
\infty
-operads: a section of
\widehat{\mathscr{C}^{\times}}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a map of operads
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\widehat{\mathscr{C}^{\times}}
over
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
, and such maps must send inert morphisms to cocartesian morphisms by
[
Lur17
, Definition 2.1.1.10]
.
∎

(To see why the condition on inert morphisms is needed we note that if
\widetilde{M}
sent
all
morphisms to cocartesian morphisms in
\widehat{\mathscr{C}^{\times}}
then the corresponding functor
M:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}
would characterize an idempotent element
M_{1}\in\mathscr{C}
, i.e.
M_{1}\times M_{1}\simeq M_{1}
.)

There is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{C}^{\times}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\otimes}}

where the top map is given on fibers by the product maps
\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}\simeq\mathscr{C}^{\times t}\to\mathscr{C}
, and the proposition informally states that

\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{t}\ni\widetilde{M}(\langle t\rangle)\simeq(M^{\otimes}_{1},M^{\otimes}_{1},\dotsc,M^{\otimes}_{1})\in\mathscr{C}^{\times t}

Proposition A.15
.

Precomposition with the smash product of finite sets
\wedge:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
promotes a commutative monoid
M^{\otimes}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})
in
\mathscr{C}
to another commutative monoid
W(M^{\otimes})\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}))
in
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})
whose carrier is equivalent to
M^{\otimes}
.

Proof.

Note that
\wedge
sends
(\langle m\rangle,\langle n\rangle)
to
\langle mn\rangle
. If we view
W(M^{\otimes})=M^{\otimes}\circ\wedge
as a functor
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}^{\times})
then the carrier is obtained by evaluating it at
\langle 1\rangle
:

W(M^{\otimes}):\langle 1\rangle\mapsto(\langle n\rangle\mapsto M^{\otimes}_{1\cdot n}=M^{\otimes}_{n})=M^{\otimes}

as desired.
∎

A.4.1.
Monoidal structures on products

Proposition A.16
.

If
M\in\mathscr{C}
admits a commutative monoidal structure (i.e. it extends to some
M^{\otimes}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})
satisfying
M^{\otimes}_{1}\simeq M
) then so does
M^{\times k}
for any
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

The smash-by-
\langle k\rangle
functor

-\wedge\langle k\rangle:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}

sends
\langle n\rangle
to
\langle nk\rangle
. In particular, it sends the Segal maps
\tau_{i}:\langle n\rangle\to\langle 1\rangle
to the maps
\kappa_{i}:\langle nk\rangle\to\langle k\rangle
defined by

\kappa_{i}(m)=\begin{cases}l-1&\text{if }m=i+lk,\\
\ast&\text{if }m\not\equiv i\mod k.\end{cases}

Precomposing
M^{\otimes}
by
-\wedge\langle k\rangle
yields a functor
N^{\otimes}:\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}
such that
N^{\otimes}_{1}\simeq M^{\times k}
and whose images fit into the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\otimes}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N^{\otimes}_{1})^{\times n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\otimes}_{nk}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{\otimes}_{k})^{\times n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\times nk}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M^{\times k})^{\times n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\kappa_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Therefore the top map, the product of the induced Segal maps, is an equivalence, which tells us that
N^{\otimes}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})
is a commutative monoid extending
N^{\otimes}_{1}\simeq M_{k}\simeq M^{\times k}
.
∎

Interestingly enough, we note that the wedge sum of pointed finite sets (which is the coproduct in that category) is precisely the symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
such that symmetric monoidal functors
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}
are exactly commutative monoids in
\mathscr{C}
for any cartesian monoidal
\mathscr{C}
:

\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}^{\otimes}((\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}},\vee),(\mathscr{C},\times)).

This fact can be used to give a shorter proof of the previous proposition.

Alternative proof of
Proposition

A.16
.

Since
\wedge
distributes over
\vee
we can directly compute that
N^{\otimes}=M^{\otimes}\circ(-\wedge\langle k\rangle)
is a symmetric monoidal functor:

\displaystyle N^{\otimes}(\langle m\rangle\vee\langle n\rangle)

\displaystyle=M^{\otimes}((\langle m\rangle\vee\langle n\rangle)\wedge\langle k\rangle)

\displaystyle=M^{\otimes}((\langle m\rangle\wedge\langle k\rangle)\vee(\langle n\rangle\wedge\langle k\rangle))

\displaystyle\simeq M^{\otimes}(\langle m\rangle\wedge\langle k\rangle)\times M^{\otimes}(\langle n\rangle\wedge\langle k\rangle)

\displaystyle\simeq N^{\otimes}(\langle m\rangle)\times N^{\otimes}(\langle n\rangle).\qed

Proposition A.17
.

If
\mathscr{C}
is a cartesian monoidal
\infty
-category then, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{n}
has finite limits and the induced symmetric monoidal structure on it is cartesian.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a finite
\infty
-category and let
D:\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{n}
be a diagram. By assumption each component
D_{i}:\mathscr{A}\to\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{n}\xlongrightarrow{(\tau_{i})_{\ast}}\mathscr{C}
has a limit
x_{i}
, and together the
x_{i}
assemble to an element
\overline{x}\in\mathscr{C}^{\times n}
corresponding to some element
x\in\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{n}
. But for any
y\in\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{n}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{n}}(y,x)\simeq\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(y_{i},x_{i})\simeq\lim_{\mathscr{A}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}\left(y_{i},D_{i}\right)\simeq\lim_{\mathscr{A}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{n}}(y,D)

and so
x
is a limit of
D
. To show that the induced monoidal structure
\mathscr{D}^{\times}
on
\mathscr{C}^{\times}_{n}
is cartesian it will suffice to show that the map
\mu_{\ast}:\mathscr{D}^{\times}_{p}\to\mathscr{D}^{\times}
, induced by the morphism
\mu:\langle p\rangle\to\langle 1\rangle
sending everything except the basepoint to
1
, is the same as the product map. But
\mu_{\ast}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\times}\circ(\mu\wedge\langle n\rangle)
by
Proposition

A.16
and each component of the latter is the product map because
\mathscr{C}
is cartesian monoidal, so we’re done.
∎

A.5.
Techniques to increase and decrease the category number

At times it will be fruitful to pass from
n
-categories to related
m
-categories for some
m>n
. Here we collect some techniques that allow us to do this.

A.5.1.
Categories of commutative
n
-cubes

We start with a generalization of Rezk’s classifying diagram (
[
Rez01
]
).

Definition A.18
.

Let
i:\mathbf{\Delta}\to\mathrm{Cat}^{1}(\mathbf{S})
denote the inclusion of the linearly ordered sets
i(k)=\Delta^{k}
via the Yoneda embedding. By taking products we get a functor
i^{n}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\times n}\to\mathrm{Cat}^{1}(\mathbf{S})
. If
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{D}
is a simplicial object in an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{D}
, the
commutative
n
-cube
of
X
is the functor

\square^{n}(X):\mathbf{\Delta}^{n,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{D},\quad(k_{1},\dotsc,k_{n})\mapsto\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}i^{n}(k_{1},\dotsc,k_{n})\pitchfork X.

First note that since
i(0)=\Delta^{0}\simeq\ast
we have

\square^{n}(X)_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{p},0,\dotsc,0}\simeq\square^{p}(X)_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{p}}

for any
p\leq n
. If
X\in\mathrm{Cat}(\mathscr{D})
, the commutative
n
-cube is supposed to classify commutative diagrams in
X
with shape the
n
-cube with sides of lengths
k_{1},k_{2},\dotsc,k_{n}
(the length here indicates the number of
1
-morphisms). We will demonstrate this by computing
\square^{2}(X)
, which is the only case used in the paper. By the equation above and the Yoneda lemma we have that

(A.5.1)

\square^{2}(X)_{k_{1},0}\simeq\square^{1}(X)_{k_{1}}\simeq\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}\Delta^{k_{1}}\pitchfork X\simeq X_{k_{1}},

i.e. the
(k_{1},0)
-simplices of
\square^{2}(X)
correspond to
k_{1}
-simplices of
X
and, since
X
is a category object, these in turn correspond to chains of
k_{1}
many composable
1
-morphisms. When
(k_{1},k_{2})=(1,1)
, the simplicial set
\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}
has exactly two non-degenerate
2
-simplices with their composite edges identified:
\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}\simeq\Delta^{2}\sqcup_{\Delta^{1}}\Delta^{2}
. Hence

(A.5.2)

\square^{2}(X)_{1,1}=\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1})\pitchfork X\simeq\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\Delta^{2}\sqcup_{\Delta^{1}}\Delta^{2})\pitchfork X\simeq X_{2}\times_{X_{1}}X_{2},

and since the map
X_{2}\to X_{1}
along which the pullback is taken is the one defining the composition in
X
we see that the
(1,1)
-simplices of
\square^{2}(X)
correspond to commutative squares of
1
-morphisms of
X
.

Proposition A.19
.

The commutative
n
-cube of
X\in\mathrm{Cat}^{1}(\mathscr{D})
, where
\mathscr{D}
is an
\infty
-category with finite limits, is an
n
-uple category object in
\mathscr{D}
.

Proof.

We will first prove the claim in the case
\mathscr{D}=\mathscr{S}
, in which case
-\pitchfork-=\mathrm{Hom}(-,-)
and so

\displaystyle\square^{n}(X)_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{i},\dotsc,k_{n}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}(\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\dotsb\times\Delta^{k_{i}}\times\dotsb\times\Delta^{k_{n}},X)

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Hom}(\Delta^{k_{i}},[\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\dotsb\times\Delta^{k_{i-1}}\times\Delta^{k_{i+1}}\times\dotsb\times\Delta^{k_{n}},X])

\displaystyle\simeq\square^{1}([\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\dotsb\times\Delta^{k_{i-1}}\times\Delta^{k_{i+1}}\times\dotsb\times\Delta^{k_{n}},X])_{k_{i}}

\displaystyle\simeq[\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\dotsb\times\Delta^{k_{i-1}}\times\Delta^{k_{i+1}}\times\dotsb\times\Delta^{k_{n}},X]_{k_{i}}

for any
1\leq i\leq n
, where
[Y,Z]
is the internal hom in simplicial spaces. If both
Y
and
Z
are category objects in spaces then
[Y,Z]\in\mathrm{Cat}^{1}(\mathscr{S})
since
-\times Y:\mathrm{Cat}^{1}(\mathscr{S})\to\mathrm{Cat}^{1}(\mathscr{S})
preserves colimits. Hence

[\Delta^{k_{1}}\times\dotsb\times\Delta^{k_{i-1}}\times\Delta^{k_{i+1}}\times\dotsb\times\Delta^{k_{n}},X]:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{S}

satisfies the Segal condition in its variable
k_{i}
, and since this holds for all
i
we are done.

When
\mathscr{D}
is a general
\infty
-category with finite limits we can reduce to the case of
\mathscr{S}
by applying
\mathscr{D}(d,-)
for an arbitrary object
d
and commuting that past the end and the cotensor, obtaining

\mathscr{D}(d,\square^{n}(X)_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{n}})\simeq\square^{n}(\mathscr{D}(d,X))_{k_{1},\dotsc,k_{n}}.

The Segal condition holds for the right-hand side by our previous argument and therefore it holds for
\square^{n}(X)
since the above equivalence is natural in
d
.
∎

A.5.2.
Monoids

Definition A.20
.

A
monoid
in a cartesian monoidal category
\mathscr{C}
is a category object
X:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{C}
with
X_{0}\simeq\ast
. The
\infty
-category of monoids in
\mathscr{C}
is the full subcategory
\mathrm{Mon}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\mathrm{Cat}(\mathscr{C})
spanned by the monoids.

A monoid
X
still comes equipped with equivalences
X_{n}\simeq X_{1}^{\times n}
but is no longer invariant under the action of the symmetric groups, so a priori the multiplication map
X_{2}\to X_{1}
is not commutative.

Proposition A.21
.

There is a functor
u:\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
such that precomposition by
u
extracts a monoid
\underline{M^{\otimes}}\in\mathrm{Mon}(\mathscr{C})
, called the
underlying monoid
, from a commutative monoid
M^{\otimes}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C})
.

Proof.

The functor
u
appears in
[
Lur17
, Construction 4.1.2.9]
: it is given on objects by
[n]\mapsto\langle n\rangle
and on morphisms by sending
\varphi:[m]\to[n]
to the map of pointed finite sets

\varphi^{\ast}:\langle n\rangle\to\langle m\rangle,\quad\varphi^{\ast}(k)=\begin{cases}\min\varphi^{-1}(k)&\text{if $\varphi^{-1}(k)\neq\emptyset$},\\
\ast&\text{if $\varphi^{-1}(k)=\emptyset$}.\end{cases}

It is easy to see that
u
sends the Segal maps in
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
to the Segal maps in
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}
, and this shows that
\underline{M^{\otimes}}=M^{\otimes}\circ u
is a monoid in
\mathscr{C}
whenever
M^{\otimes}
is a commutative monoid in
\mathscr{C}
.
∎

Corollary A.22
.

The underlying monoid of a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category is an
(\infty,2)
-category with a contractible space of objects.

Proof.

A symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category is, by definition, a commutative monoid
\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1})
. Recall that
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1}
can be modeled by a special subcategory of
\mathrm{Seg}^{1}(\mathscr{S})
. So we have an inclusion
\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{1})\subseteq\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathrm{Seg}^{1}(\mathscr{S}))
and, by adjunction, we can consider
\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}
as a functor
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{S}
which satisfies the Segal condition in both variables. Precomposing with
u
in the first variable yields a
2
-fold Segal space
\underline{\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}}:\mathbf{\Delta}^{2,{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathscr{S}
with a contractible space of objects since
\underline{\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}}_{0,n}\simeq\mathscr{C}^{\otimes}_{0,n}\simeq\ast
for any
n
. After completing (i.e. applying
\mathcal{U}^{2}
) we obtain the desired
(\infty,2)
-category.
∎
